## Mark 9:43 (id: 21632)

hell: Greek *Gehenna,* referring to the Valley of Hinnom, which bordered Jerusalem on the southwest. It was a garbage dump for the city, and the continual burning of refuse there became a metaphor for the final place of judgment for the wicked.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 9:43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hell (ID: `place:Hell`); Cause to Stumble (ID: `keyterm:CauseToStumble`); Gehenna (ID: `keyterm:Gehenna`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`)

## Mark 9:43–48 (id: 21631)

Most of the best manuscripts do not include [9:44](https://ref.ly/Mark9:44), [46](https://ref.ly/Mark9:46), but a few include them. These two verses were almost certainly copied from [9:48](https://ref.ly/Mark9:48) and were not in the original text of Mark.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 9:43-48

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cause to Stumble (ID: `keyterm:CauseToStumble`); Hell (ID: `place:Hell`); Gehenna (ID: `keyterm:Gehenna`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Worm (ID: `fauna:Maggot`)

## Mark 9:48 (id: 21633)

The eternality of hell is emphasized by the phrases never die and never goes out (cp. [9:43](https://ref.ly/Mark9:43)). The horror of hell is emphasized by its portrayal as a place of everlasting fire, and of decay and corruption where maggots eternally eat everything away. The putrid smell of decay and the presence of maggots in the Valley of Hinnom may lie behind this imagery (see study note on 9:43). This imagery is a powerful warning for people to repent in order to escape the punishment of hell.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 9:48
* **Associated Articles:** Mark 9:43 (ID: `21632@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Worm (ID: `fauna:Maggot`)

## Mark 9:49–50 (id: 21634)

tested with fire: Literally *salted with fire.* The fire of testing has a purifying effect, like salt (see also [1 Pet 1:7](https://ref.ly/1Pet1:7); [4:12](https://ref.ly/1Pet4:12); [Rev 3:18](https://ref.ly/Rev3:18)). Salt also refers to Christian character. Genuine Christian character will have a genuinely purifying influence. • But if it loses its flavor: The salt mined from the Dead Sea often contained gypsum, so although it looked like perfectly good salt, it was useless. It did not taste salty, and it created a disposal problem.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 9:49-50

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be at Peace (ID: `keyterm:AtPeace`)

## Mark 10:1–2 (id: 21222)

The Pharisees’ question about divorce was much debated in Judaism, but it had a hostile purpose as they tried to trap Jesus (see [2:16](https://ref.ly/Mark2:16), [18](https://ref.ly/Mark2:18), [24](https://ref.ly/Mark2:24); [7:5](https://ref.ly/Mark7:5); [8:11](https://ref.ly/Mark8:11); [12:13](https://ref.ly/Mark12:13)). John the Baptist was beheaded over his teaching that Herod Antipas’s divorce and remarriage was unlawful ([6:18–19](https://ref.ly/Mark6:18-Mark6:19)), and according to the Jewish historian Josephus, John was martyred close to Jesus’ current location east of the Jordan River, at Herod Antipas’s fortress at Machaerus (see [6:28](https://ref.ly/Mark6:28); Josephus, *Antiquities* 18\.5\.2\). If Jesus answered in agreement with John the Baptist, the Pharisees could indict him before Herod. But if Jesus said that divorce was lawful, he would be contradicting a prophet.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judea (ID: `place:Judea`); Be Lawful (ID: `keyterm:Lawful`); Jordan River (ID: `place:Jordan`)

## Mark 10:3 (id: 21249)

Jesus answered the trick question of the Pharisees with a counter\-question. Instead of replying directly, he asked them a question in return (see [Matthew 22:20](https://ref.ly/Matt22:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Command (ID: `keyterm:Command`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`)

## Mark 10:4 (id: 21257)

The Pharisees quoted what Moses permitted. There was much debate among rabbis as to what constituted the “something wrong” (see [Deut 24:1](https://ref.ly/Deut24:1)) that made divorce permissible. Rabbi Shammai allowed divorce only on the basis of sexual immorality. Rabbi Hillel permitted divorce even if a woman burned her husband’s dinner or was less attractive than someone else (*Mishnah Gittin* 9:10\).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`)

## Mark 10:5–9 (id: 21258)

God permitted divorce as a concession to the hard hearts of the people. But God’s will is more aptly expressed in the passages that Jesus quotes from the law of Moses ([Gen 1:27](https://ref.ly/Gen1:27); [2:23–24](https://ref.ly/Gen2:23-Gen2:24); see also [Mal 2:16](https://ref.ly/Mal2:16)). Jesus shows that God delights in marriage, which is the creation of a new union in which two become one. No one should rebel against God’s will by seeking to split apart what God has united.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:5-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hardheartedness (ID: `keyterm:Hardheartedness`); Body (ID: `keyterm:Body.3`); Commandment (ID: `keyterm:Commandment`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 10:10 (id: 21223)

Jesus often explained his teaching to his disciples in the privacy of a house (see [7:17](https://ref.ly/Mark7:17); [9:28](https://ref.ly/Mark9:28), [33](https://ref.ly/Mark9:33)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Mark 10:11–12 (id: 21224)

Whoever divorces . . . and marries someone else commits adultery: The parallel in [Luke 16:18](https://ref.ly/Luke16:18) agrees with Mark and mentions no exceptions to this prohibition of divorce, while Matthew’s parallel account allows an exception in cases of infidelity ([Matt 19:9](https://ref.ly/Matt19:9); see also [Matt 5:32](https://ref.ly/Matt5:32)). Paul also allows an exception if an unbelieving partner deserts the marriage ([1 Cor 7:15](https://ref.ly/1Cor7:15)). Mark’s account focuses on the core principles—God hates divorce ([Mal 2:16](https://ref.ly/Mal2:16)), marriage is meant to be for life, and divorce betrays the divine purpose of marriage.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:11-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`)

## Mark 10:13 (id: 21226)

The ages of the children cannot be determined from this passage. The Greek word paidia can refer to a broad spectrum of ages, from a twelve\-year\-old child ([5:41–42](https://ref.ly/Mark5:41-Mark5:42)) down to an eight\-day\-old infant ([Gen 17:12](https://ref.ly/Gen17:12), Greek version). • the disciples scolded the parents: See study note on Luke 18:15–17.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:13
* **Associated Articles:** Luke 18:15-17 (ID: `20662@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); To Command (ID: `keyterm:Command`)

## Mark 10:13–16 (id: 21225)

Jesus’ love and concern for children has already been seen in [5:41–43](https://ref.ly/Mark5:41-Mark5:43); [9:36–37](https://ref.ly/Mark9:36-Mark9:37), [42](https://ref.ly/Mark9:42). Jesus uses the incident to teach that the Kingdom of God belongs to those who are like these children.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:13-16

Mark does not explain what characteristics of children make them fit for the Kingdom of God*;* [Matthew 18:4–5](https://ref.ly/Matt18:4-Matt18:5) suggests that the attributes include humility and the ability to receive things simply.

## Mark 10:17 (id: 21229)

The rich man ([10:22](https://ref.ly/Mark10:22)) was a young ruler ([Matt 19:20](https://ref.ly/Matt19:20); [Luke 18:18](https://ref.ly/Luke18:18)) who showed respect toward Jesus by kneeling and calling Jesus Good Teacher. *Teacher* is a frequent title for Jesus in Mark (see [Mark 4:38](https://ref.ly/Mark4:38); [5:35](https://ref.ly/Mark5:35); [9:17](https://ref.ly/Mark9:17), [38](https://ref.ly/Mark9:38); [10:20](https://ref.ly/Mark10:20), [35](https://ref.ly/Mark10:35); [12:14](https://ref.ly/Mark12:14), [19](https://ref.ly/Mark12:19), [32](https://ref.ly/Mark12:32); [13:1](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1); [14:14](https://ref.ly/Mark14:14)); the addition of *good* is unique.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Always (ID: `keyterm:Always`); Good (ID: `keyterm:Good`); Rich Young Ruler (ID: `person:RichYoungRuler`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`)

## Mark 10:17–31 (id: 21228)

The story of the rich man continues the themes of discipleship begun in [9:33](https://ref.ly/Mark9:33) and the requirements for entering the Kingdom of God ([10:13–16](https://ref.ly/Mark10:13-Mark10:16)). The attitude of the rich man contrasts sharply with the childlike faith necessary for entering the Kingdom of God.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:17-31

## Mark 10:18 (id: 21230)

Jesus’ reply has troubled interpreters for centuries. Jesus was probably objecting to the man’s loose application of the term *good* to any human being, since ultimate goodness and perfection belong to God alone. Without in any way denying his own goodness, Jesus wanted to focus the rich man’s attention on God. The rich man wanted to enter the Kingdom of God, and Jesus was contrasting God’s goodness with the man’s own human sinfulness.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Good (ID: `keyterm:Good`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 10:19 (id: 21231)

Jesus’ reply seems strange to Christians who are familiar with Paul’s teaching on this subject (e.g., [Rom 3:20](https://ref.ly/Rom3:20); [Gal 2:16](https://ref.ly/Gal2:16)). In naming five of the Ten Commandments Jesus was not implying that the man could earn eternal life by keeping them; he was telling him what God’s standards are and allowing the man to evaluate his own performance. Truly loving God with all one’s heart, soul, mind, and strength, and one’s neighbor as one’s self ([Mark 12:30–31](https://ref.ly/Mark12:30-Mark12:31)), requires trusting in God’s grace, accepting Jesus’ sacrificial death on our behalf ([10:45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:45); [14:24](https://ref.ly/Mark14:24)), and keeping his commands ([John 15:10](https://ref.ly/John15:10); [1 Jn 2:4](https://ref.ly/1John2:4); [3:22](https://ref.ly/1John3:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); Commandment (ID: `keyterm:Commandment`)

## Mark 10:20 (id: 21232)

The man’s reply was naive (as to what it really means to keep these commands; see [Matt 5:21–48](https://ref.ly/Matt5:21-Matt5:48)), but it was not arrogant, as the lack of any rebuke indicates (cp. [Mark 12:13–17](https://ref.ly/Mark12:13-Mark12:17); [Matt 23:1–36](https://ref.ly/Matt23:1-Matt23:36)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rich Young Ruler (ID: `person:RichYoungRuler`)

## Mark 10:21 (id: 21233)

Instead of having an extended discussion about what it means to truly keep the commandments, Jesus focused on the specific issue that revealed this man’s problem. He still lacked one thing: He loved riches more than he loved God, thus breaking the first and most important commandment ([12:29–30](https://ref.ly/Mark12:29-Mark12:30); [Exod 20:3](https://ref.ly/Exod20:3); [Deut 6:5](https://ref.ly/Deut6:5)). Entering the Kingdom of God requires repentance ([Mark 1:15](https://ref.ly/Mark1:15); [6:12](https://ref.ly/Mark6:12)), and Jesus helped this man to understand exactly what repentance entailed for him. He did not need, as he might have thought, to attain a higher level of personal righteousness. He needed to enter God’s Kingdom through repentance and wholehearted love for God, thereby obtaining eternal life. He must deny himself ([8:34](https://ref.ly/Mark8:34)) and love God first and foremost by giving away his money. For other commands involving a negative \[sell all] and a positive \[follow me] element, see [1:15](https://ref.ly/Mark1:15), [18](https://ref.ly/Mark1:18), [20](https://ref.ly/Mark1:20); [10:28](https://ref.ly/Mark10:28); see also [Acts 2:38](https://ref.ly/Acts2:38); [20:21](https://ref.ly/Acts20:21). • Jesus’ command to the rich man was not a universal requirement for entering the Kingdom of God but was addressed to his particular situation. It teaches anyone seeking eternal life that nothing else can come before God; repentance ([Mark 1:15](https://ref.ly/Mark1:15); [6:12](https://ref.ly/Mark6:12)) requires the removal of anything that we place above God.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`)

## Mark 10:22 (id: 21234)

Mark records the rich man’s tragic choice. The man departed, still possessing his earthly riches ([Matt 6:19](https://ref.ly/Matt6:19)) but lacking eternal treasure ([Matt 13:44–46](https://ref.ly/Matt13:44-Matt13:46)). His demeanor (face fell . . . sad) showed his awareness that his awful choice had brought him sorrow rather than joy.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:22

Jesus surprised the disciples by challenging the common belief that wealth was a sign of God's favor.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:23-27

## Mark 10:24 (id: 21236)

When the disciples were amazed, Jesus spoke to them with care and called them *children*.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 10:25 (id: 21237)

Jesus was emphatically warning that riches are an obstacle to entering the Kingdom of God. The camel was the largest animal in Palestine, the eye of a needle the smallest hole. Some rich people are evidently able to overcome the problem created by riches and follow Jesus, but Jesus’ followers came from among the poor more than from the rich. • Explanations about a gate in the Jerusalem wall called the “Eye of the Needle” are ill\-conceived. There never was such a gate, and this explanation loses sight of Jesus’ frequent use of hyperbole in his teaching (see also [Matt 7:3–5](https://ref.ly/Matt7:3-Matt7:5); [23:24](https://ref.ly/Matt23:24)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Needle (ID: `realia:Needle`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`)

## Mark 10:26 (id: 21238)

The disciples were astounded: It was generally believed that wealth was a sign of God’s blessing that enabled the rich to do good deeds. Accordingly, it was assumed that the rich had an inside track on entering the Kingdom of God. The disciples’ question was natural, given this understanding. God’s assessment was the opposite of their conventional wisdom.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)

## Mark 10:27 (id: 21239)

This verse tends to be interpreted in one of two ways: (1\) Although salvation by one’s own effort is impossible, by God’s grace people can be saved through faith ([Eph 2:8–10](https://ref.ly/Eph2:8-Eph2:10)). (2\) God can give an unusual grace that enables people to overcome their sinful love of riches and believe. Mark does not explain his understanding.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 10:28 (id: 21240)

The disciples had done what Jesus told the rich man to do. They had given up everything to follow Jesus ([1:16–20](https://ref.ly/Mark1:16-Mark1:20); [8:34–38](https://ref.ly/Mark8:34-Mark8:38)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Peter (ID: `person:Peter`)

## Mark 10:29–30 (id: 21241)

Jesus assured his disciples that they would receive whatever they had given up for him many times over, including a new family in Christ (brothers*,* sisters*,* mothers*,* children) and Christian hospitality (houses). • a hundred times: What is gained in following Jesus far outweighs any loss. And in the world to come they will inherit the eternal life that the rich man desired but did not receive.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:29-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Surely! (ID: `keyterm:Surely`); Good News (ID: `keyterm:GoodNews`); Always (ID: `keyterm:Always`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`)

## Mark 10:31 (id: 21242)

But many . . . greatest then: The account ends with a proverb, contrasting the way that God understands life and how people generally understand it (for similar sayings, see [Matt 20:16](https://ref.ly/Matt20:16); [Luke 13:30](https://ref.ly/Luke13:30)). For those with eyes to see and ears to hear ([Mark 4:9–12](https://ref.ly/Mark4:9-Mark4:12); [8:18](https://ref.ly/Mark8:18)), the Kingdom of God has already come, and the overturning of this world’s values has begun.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:31


## Mark 10:32–34 (id: 21243)

The disciples’ awe and the people’s fear cast a dark shadow over what lay ahead, given the hostility of the Jerusalem leaders toward Jesus (see [3:22–30](https://ref.ly/Mark3:22-Mark3:30); [7:1–13](https://ref.ly/Mark7:1-Mark7:13)). Taking the disciples aside again, Jesus described the coming events in the greatest detail yet (see also [8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31); [9:31](https://ref.ly/Mark9:31)). He knew what was about to happen; what awaited him in Jerusalem was neither a tragedy nor fate, but God’s will (see [8:31–33](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31-Mark8:33); [Acts 4:27–28](https://ref.ly/Acts4:27-Acts4:28)). As the Son of God, Jesus had unique knowledge of his upcoming unique death as the Savior of the world.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:32-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Condemn (ID: `keyterm:Condemn`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`)

## Mark 10:33 (id: 21244)

The leading priests and the teachers of religious law were the human agents who fulfilled God’s purpose through their hatred of Jesus. • They did not have the right of capital punishment ([John 18:31](https://ref.ly/John18:31)), so they had to hand Jesus over to the Romans to carry out the actual execution.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:33

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); Condemn (ID: `keyterm:Condemn`)

## Mark 10:35–45 (id: 21245)

Following Jesus’ third prediction of his suffering ([10:32–33](https://ref.ly/Mark10:32-Mark10:33)) comes another example of the disciples’ failure (see [8:31–33](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31-Mark8:33); [9:31–34](https://ref.ly/Mark9:31-Mark9:34)). The first part of the account involves the foolish request of James and John ([10:35–37](https://ref.ly/Mark10:35-Mark10:37)) and Jesus’ reply to them ([10:38–40](https://ref.ly/Mark10:38-Mark10:40)). Then Jesus explains to the other disciples what greatness and leadership in the Kingdom of God mean ([10:41–44](https://ref.ly/Mark10:41-Mark10:44)). Jesus’ own supreme example of servanthood ([10:45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:45)) illustrates and concludes this account.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:35-45

## Mark 10:37 (id: 21246)

The brothers’ request indicates that James and John correctly understood that Jesus was the Messiah (see [8:29](https://ref.ly/Mark8:29); [9:2–8](https://ref.ly/Mark9:2-Mark9:8)). However, they completely misunderstood what it meant to be a leader in God’s Kingdom ([10:41–45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:41-Mark10:45)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:37

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`)

## Mark 10:38 (id: 21247)

The two brothers didn’t know what they were asking: To share in his glory, they must share in his suffering as servants. • to drink from the bitter cup: Drinking from a cup is often associated with suffering and death ([Ps 75:8](https://ref.ly/Ps75:8); [Isa 51:17](https://ref.ly/Isa51:17), [22](https://ref.ly/Isa51:22); [Jer 25:15](https://ref.ly/Jer25:15); [49:12](https://ref.ly/Jer49:12); [Lam 4:21](https://ref.ly/Lam4:21); see also [Mark 14:24](https://ref.ly/Mark14:24), [36](https://ref.ly/Mark14:36)). • The baptism of suffering recalls Jesus’ total commitment to God’s calling at his baptism, and it speaks of the believer’s own baptism into Jesus’ death, burial, and resurrection ([Rom 6:3–5](https://ref.ly/Rom6:3-Rom6:5); [Col 2:12](https://ref.ly/Col2:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:38

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Baptism (ID: `keyterm:Baptism`)

## Mark 10:39 (id: 21248)

James and John did indeed drink a bitter cup . . . of suffering for Jesus. James died for his faith ([Acts 12:1–5](https://ref.ly/Acts12:1-Acts12:5)). John died an old man, having experienced persecution (Tertullian, *The Soul* 50; Jerome, *Commentary on Galatians* 6, 10\). Yet the death of James and John was not the same as that of Jesus—no believer can die Jesus’ death as a vicarious sacrifice for the sins of the world ([Mark 10:45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:45)) or experience the divine wrath as he did ([15:34](https://ref.ly/Mark15:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:39

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Baptism (ID: `keyterm:Baptism`)

## Mark 10:40 (id: 21250)

Jesus did not grant their request. These places belong to those for whom God the Father has prepared them (see also [13:32](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:40


## Mark 10:41 (id: 21251)

*the ten … became indignant*: Perhaps they wanted the special places requested by James and John for themselves. All of the disciples needed a new understanding of what leadership in God’s kingdom means ([10:42–45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:42-Mark10:45)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:41

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** John (ID: `person:John.2`); James (ID: `person:James`)

## Mark 10:42–44 (id: 21252)

*the rulers of the Gentiles*: The unbelieving world (the rulers in this world) thinks that leadership means dominating others. Just as the role of Jesus as Messiah and Son of God meant suffering and death ([8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31); [9:31](https://ref.ly/Mark9:31); [10:32–34](https://ref.ly/Mark10:32-Mark10:34), [45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:45)), being his follower involves serving others, not ruling over them ([9:35](https://ref.ly/Mark9:35); [John 10:11](https://ref.ly/John10:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:42-44

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Authority (ID: `keyterm:Authority.2`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`)

## Mark 10:45 (id: 21253)

Jesus offered his own example to demonstrate what leadership in God’s Kingdom means. • Jesus understands his death as a ransom. A ransom was a payment made to free a slave or captive. • The expression for many is probably an allusion to [Isa 53:12](https://ref.ly/Isa53:12), where *many* means *all* (cp. [Rom 5:15](https://ref.ly/Rom5:15), [18–20](https://ref.ly/Rom5:18-Rom5:20)). Jesus died as the substitute for all. Later, Jesus would speak of his blood as being poured out for many ([Mark 14:24](https://ref.ly/Mark14:24)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:45

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Ransom (ID: `keyterm:Ransom`); Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`)

## Mark 10:46 (id: 21255)

they reached Jericho: The journey Jesus began in [10:1](https://ref.ly/Mark10:1) was nearing its conclusion. Jericho was the last major city in the Jordan River Valley before Jerusalem. It may be the oldest continually occupied city in the world. In Jesus’ day, it was no longer located on the much smaller site of Old Testament times (Tell es\-Sultan) but had been moved and greatly enlarged. The road from Jericho up to Jerusalem was steep, with a difference in elevation of about 3,000 feet (about 1,000 meters). • The large crowd following Jesus might have been fellow pilgrims traveling to Jerusalem for Passover, but they certainly show Jesus’ great popularity and charisma. • A blind beggar . . . was sitting beside the road: This was a good location for collecting alms from religious pilgrims going to Jerusalem.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:46

## Mark 10:46–52 (id: 21254)

The healing of blind Bartimaeus is the last healing miracle recorded in Mark. This event and the healing of the blind man in [8:22–26](https://ref.ly/Mark8:22-Mark8:26) form bookends around this section (see Mark Book Introduction, “Literary Features”). It is also a bridge to Jesus’ entrance into Jerusalem as Israel’s Messiah in [11:1–11](https://ref.ly/Mark11:1-Mark11:11). Bartimaeus’s confession (Jesus, Son of David) prepares the reader for the confession of the people upon Jesus’ arrival in Jerusalem ([11:10](https://ref.ly/Mark11:10)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:46-52

## Mark 10:47–48 (id: 21256)

Bartimaeus heard: Jesus’ great fame had spread to Jericho, even as it had spread to Gentile areas ([3:8](https://ref.ly/Mark3:8)). • Jesus, Son of David: Mark does not explain how Bartimaeus knew of Jesus’ Davidic ancestry. Perhaps he knew that Jesus was the Messiah. The blind man, however, did not here associate this title with political or military goals as did the vast majority of people in his day. Instead, Bartimaeus focused on the fact that Jesus was concerned with bringing God’s Kingdom to the poor, maimed, lame, and blind, and he requested mercy and healing ([10:51](https://ref.ly/Mark10:51))—which was in harmony with Jesus’ own understanding (cp. [Luke 4:18–19](https://ref.ly/Luke4:18-Luke4:19)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 10:47-48

threw aside his coat: Bartimaeus’s response is best understood as simply a spontaneous act of joy and anticipation rather than as a symbolic casting off of his old way of life or leaving all to follow Jesus.

The man’s faith was rewarded, and he was healed (literally *saved*; see [5:23](https://ref.ly/Mark5:23), [28](https://ref.ly/Mark5:28), [34](https://ref.ly/Mark5:34); [6:56](https://ref.ly/Mark6:56); [Matt 9:22](https://ref.ly/Matt9:22); [Luke 8:48](https://ref.ly/Luke8:48)). ## Mark 11:1 (id: 21272)

Jerusalem was Jesus’ goal. During the week before his crucifixion, Jesus apparently stayed in Bethany with his disciples ([11:11–12](https://ref.ly/Mark11:11-Mark11:12); [14:3–9](https://ref.ly/Mark14:3-Mark14:9)). • The Mount of Olives is frequently associated with the site of the final judgment ([Zech 14:4](https://ref.ly/Zech14:4)) and the place where the Messiah will manifest himself ([Ezek 11:23](https://ref.ly/Ezek11:23); [43:1–5](https://ref.ly/Ezek43:1-Ezek43:5); see also Josephus, *Antiquities* 20\.8\.6\). • As he did at other times, Jesus sent two of them (see [Mark 6:7](https://ref.ly/Mark6:7)).

## Mark 11:1–13.37 (id: 21262)

This section centers on Jesus’ relationship to the Jerusalem Temple. Mark’s geographical arrangement places in [11:1–16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark11:1-Mark16:8) all his accounts of Jesus’ teachings and events associated with Jerusalem. • The section concludes ([13:1–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1-Mark13:37)) with Jesus’ second extended teaching discourse (see [4:1–34](https://ref.ly/Mark4:1-Mark4:34)), now focusing on the destruction of the Temple and the coming of the Son of Man. It is the climax for numerous statements within [11:1–13:37](https://ref.ly/Mark11:1-Mark13:37) concerning the divine judgment about to fall on Jerusalem and the Temple (see especially [11:12–25](https://ref.ly/Mark11:12-Mark11:25) and [12:1–12](https://ref.ly/Mark12:1-Mark12:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:1-25

## Mark 11:9–10 (id: 21286)

Praise God! (Greek *Hosanna*): By the first century AD, *Hosanna* was no longer used literally as a cry for rescue from enemies but had become an idiomatic expression of joy and jubilation (cp. “Praise the Lord!” as used today). For the people, it was the exuberant welcome ([Luke 19:38–40](https://ref.ly/Luke19:38-Luke19:40)) of a famous pilgrim, the prophet from Galilee ([Matt 21:11](https://ref.ly/Matt21:11)). The miracle of raising Lazarus from the dead might have contributed to the excitement of the day ([John 12:9–11](https://ref.ly/John12:9-John12:11)), but even the disciples did not truly understand this event until later ([John 12:16](https://ref.ly/John12:16)). For Jesus, though, it was a fulfillment of prophecy. Jesus prepared for the occasion carefully and offered himself to the people of Israel as God’s Anointed, the Messiah.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:9-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hosanna (ID: `keyterm:Hosanna`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); A Group of People (ID: `group:GenericGroup`); Height (ID: `keyterm:Height`); David (ID: `person:David`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 11:11 (id: 21263)

The account ends surprisingly: The exuberant crowd disappears and Jesus’ actual entrance into the Temple is anticlimactic in comparison to his approach. Apparently, in the minds of the people, nothing important had taken place. Jesus’ looking around prepares the reader for God’s judgment on the Temple ([11:15–17](https://ref.ly/Mark11:15-Mark11:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Bethany (ID: `place:Bethany`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Mark 11:12–25 (id: 21264)

In the synoptic Gospels, Jesus’ messianic entry into Jerusalem is intimately associated with the cleansing of the Temple (see [11:15–17](https://ref.ly/Mark11:15-Mark11:17); cp. [Matt 21:1–17](https://ref.ly/Matt21:1-Matt21:17); [Luke 19:28–48](https://ref.ly/Luke19:28-Luke19:48)). In John, however, the account of the cleansing is recorded early in Jesus’ ministry ([John 2:13–22](https://ref.ly/John2:13-John2:22)). It is uncertain whether there were two separate cleansings of the Temple (an early event recorded in John and this later event recorded in Matthew, Mark, and Luke) or just one cleansing recorded at different places by John and the synoptic writers. • In Mark’s account, the cursing of the fig tree ([Mark 11:12–14](https://ref.ly/Mark11:12-Mark11:14), [20–25](https://ref.ly/Mark11:20-Mark11:25)) forms an *inclusio* (bookends) around the cleansing of the Temple ([11:15–19](https://ref.ly/Mark11:15-Mark11:19)). Mark thus indicates that Jesus’ cleansing of the Temple should be interpreted in light of his cursing of the fig tree—as an act of judgment rather than reformation.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:12-25

(ID: `keyterm:Surely`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Heart (ID: `keyterm:Heart`); Peter (ID: `person:Peter`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Always (ID: `keyterm:Always`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Seat (ID: `realia:Seat`); Dove (ID: `fauna:Dove`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Mark 11:13–14 (id: 21265)

it was too early in the season for fruit: Jesus did not curse the fig tree merely because it lacked fruit. Rather, he was performing a prophetic sign act like those of earlier prophets ([Isa 20](https://ref.ly/Isa20:1-Isa20:6); [Jer 13:1–11](https://ref.ly/Jer13:1-Jer13:11); [19:1–13](https://ref.ly/Jer19:1-Jer19:13); [Ezek 4](https://ref.ly/Ezek4:1-Ezek4:17)). Just as Jesus judged the fruitless fig tree, so too he judged the Temple and the worship of Israel, in which he found no fruit. This judgment was fulfilled in AD 70 with the destruction of Jerusalem and the Temple by the Romans.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:13-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Always (ID: `keyterm:Always`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`)

## Mark 11:15–16 (id: 21266)

When they returned to the temple, Jesus was very upset about the buying and selling of sacrificial animals and the money exchange. It was supervised by priests, who alone could certify that a potential sacrificial offering was acceptable. Selling animals for sacrifices guaranteed that they only used animals that had no imperfections in temple services. 

They placed exchange tables throughout Jerusalem and inside the temple. They did this on the fifteenth of Adar, the month before Passover. The exchange rate usually had a 4\- to 8\-percent fee. Because of the large number of half\-shekels (see [Exodus 30:11–16](https://ref.ly/Exod30:11-Exod30:16)) and sacrifices involved, the money exchange and the sale of sacrificial animals was quite profitable. 

Jewish writings of the time criticized priests for gaining wealth by taking advantage of poor people. Jesus did not oppose the temple or the system of sacrifices ([Mark 1:44](https://ref.ly/Mark1:44); [Matthew 5:17–19](https://ref.ly/Matt5:17-Matt5:19), [23](https://ref.ly/Matt5:23); [17:24–27](https://ref.ly/Matt17:24-Matt17:27); [Luke 17:14](https://ref.ly/Luke17:14); [Acts 6:7](https://ref.ly/Acts6:7); [21:20–26](https://ref.ly/Acts21:20-Acts21:26)). But he saw this profit\-making as theft. It did not honor the temple as a holy place. 

The actions of Jesus likely affected fewer people than many think. These actions occurred in one area of the large Court of the Gentiles. Most people in other parts of the temple did not see it happen. 

The lack of interference by the Roman authorities suggests that they did not observe the event or did not regard it as a riot or a political rebellion. At the trial of Jesus, they did not mention this action. It was not a big attempt to stop all activities like this, but it was a symbolic act pointing to the judgment that was coming upon the temple and the city.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:15-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Seat (ID: `realia:Seat`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Dove (ID: `fauna:Dove`)

## Mark 11:15–19 (id: 21267)

The largest area of the temple in Jerusalem was the Court of the Gentiles. Covered porches surrounded it. This was probably where the selling took place.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:15-19

## Mark 11:16 (id: 21268)

*to carry merchandise through the temple courts*: People also misused the temple by using it as a quick way to get from one part of the city to another. Other rabbis also forbade this practice (see *Mishnah Berakhot* 9\.5; see Josephus, *Against Apion* 2\.8\).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Mark 11:17 (id: 21269)

Jesus quoted [Isa 56:7](https://ref.ly/Isa56:7) and [Jer 7:11](https://ref.ly/Jer7:11)in defense of his action. The first quotation emphasizes the importance of the Temple for Gentiles. God’s house was intended by its very architecture (i.e., the Court of the Gentiles) to assist Gentiles in becoming children of Abraham. Jesus’ act of judgment fell upon the leaders of Israel for hindering the Gentiles’ access to the Temple. They were desecrating the Temple by taking part of the Temple specifically designated as a house of prayer for all nations and turning it into a den of thieves through the profiteering taking place.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Mark 11:18 (id: 21270)

The leading priests and teachers of religious law plotted to kill Jesus because they feared him. His great influence among the people, the amazement created by his actions, and the influence of his teaching threatened their position (see [3:6](https://ref.ly/Mark3:6); [12:12](https://ref.ly/Mark12:12); [14:1–2](https://ref.ly/Mark14:1-Mark14:2), [10–11](https://ref.ly/Mark14:10-Mark14:11)). Their response shows that reform was not enough: God’s judgment was necessary.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`)

## Mark 11:19 (id: 21271)

*Jesus and His disciples went out*: They likely went back to Bethany ([11:11](https://ref.ly/Mark11:11); [14:3](https://ref.ly/Mark14:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:19


## Mark 11:20–21 (id: 21274)

Jesus’ miraculous power to judge the fig tree testified to his authority to bring about the judgment of Jerusalem that he had foretold. If Mark’s original audience was hearing his Gospel read to them in the late AD 60s, they knew that Roman legions were already enacting this prophetic judgment against Jerusalem.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:20-21

## Mark 11:22–23 (id: 21275)

The importance of Jesus’ words is emphasized by his emphatic I tell you the truth. • This mountain probably indicates the Temple Mount, not the Mount of Olives. The judgment of the Temple is the focus of [11:12–25](https://ref.ly/Mark11:12-Mark11:25); it will be referred to again in [12:9](https://ref.ly/Mark12:9) and at great length in [13:1–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1-Mark13:37). Jesus’ reference to destroying the Temple would later be raised at his trial ([14:58](https://ref.ly/Mark14:58)) and crucifixion ([15:29](https://ref.ly/Mark15:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:22-23

## Mark 11:24–25 (id: 21276)

Two additional sayings on prayer are tied to [11:23](https://ref.ly/Mark11:23) by the word believe. In the first ([11:24](https://ref.ly/Mark11:24)), we are told that we will receive what we ask for (see [Matt 7:7](https://ref.ly/Matt7:7)) if we pray with the faith described in [Mark 11:23](https://ref.ly/Mark11:23). • Forgiveness is the second condition for answered prayer. Only when we forgive others can our most important prayer, that God forgive us, be answered ([Matt 5:23–24](https://ref.ly/Matt5:23-Matt5:24); [6:12](https://ref.ly/Matt6:12), [14–15](https://ref.ly/Matt6:14-Matt6:15); [Luke 6:37](https://ref.ly/Luke6:37); [11:4](https://ref.ly/Luke11:4); cp. [Luke 7:41–43](https://ref.ly/Luke7:41-Luke7:43), [47](https://ref.ly/Luke7:47); [Eph 4:32](https://ref.ly/Eph4:32); [Col 3:13](https://ref.ly/Col3:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:24-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 11:27 (id: 21278)

Upon returning again to Jerusalem (see [11:1](https://ref.ly/Mark11:1), [11](https://ref.ly/Mark11:11), [15](https://ref.ly/Mark11:15)), Jesus and the disciples entered the Temple. The frequent repetition of Jerusalem reminds readers of what would soon take place there ([10:32–34](https://ref.ly/Mark10:32-Mark10:34); see [8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31); [9:31](https://ref.ly/Mark9:31); [14:1–16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark14:1-Mark16:8)). • In the Temple, Jesus encountered the leading priests, the teachers of religious law, and the elders. These three groups made up the seventy\-one–member high council (the ruling body of the nation). From earlier encounters with them ([8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31); see also [14:43](https://ref.ly/Mark14:43), [53](https://ref.ly/Mark14:53); [15:1](https://ref.ly/Mark15:1)), we expect them to oppose Jesus.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Mark 11:27–33 (id: 21277)

Jesus cleansed the temple ([11:15–17](https://ref.ly/Mark11:15-Mark11:17); [Matthew 21:12–22](https://ref.ly/Matt21:12-Matt21:22); [Luke 19:45–46](https://ref.ly/Luke19:45-Luke19:46)). Then his opponents questioned his authority to do so (see also [Matt 21:23–27](https://ref.ly/Matt21:23-Matt21:27); [Luke 20:1–8](https://ref.ly/Luke20:1-Luke20:8); compare [John 2:18–22](https://ref.ly/John2:18-John2:22)).

## Mark 11:28 (id: 21279)

Jesus has authority to: 

* drive out demons ([1:22](https://ref.ly/Mark1:22), [27](https://ref.ly/Mark1:27); [5:1–13](https://ref.ly/Mark5:1-Mark5:13)),
* heal ([1:29–2:12](https://ref.ly/Mark1:29-Mark2:12); [5:21–43](https://ref.ly/Mark5:21-Mark5:43)),
* forgive sins ([2:10](https://ref.ly/Mark2:10)), and
* cleanse the temple.

His authority came from God, but the religious leaders refused to accept it.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Authority (ID: `keyterm:Authority.2`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`)

## Mark 11:29–30 (id: 21280)

Jesus often responded to his opponents' questions with other questions (for example, [2:8–9](https://ref.ly/Mark2:8-Mark2:9), [19](https://ref.ly/Mark2:19), [25–26](https://ref.ly/Mark2:25-Mark2:26); [3:4](https://ref.ly/Mark3:4), [23](https://ref.ly/Mark3:23); [10:3](https://ref.ly/Mark10:3), [38](https://ref.ly/Mark10:38); see also [12:16](https://ref.ly/Mark12:16)). This is an effective way both to teach and to refute.

## Mark 11:31–32 (id: 21281)

Jesus clearly placed his opponents in a bind, for either a “yes” or a “no” would damage their reputation and authority in the eyes of the people. Denying John’s prophetic authority would probably anger the people. Affirming it would mean that they should have accepted what John said, including what he said about Jesus. This answer would also answer their own question ([11:28](https://ref.ly/Mark11:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:31-32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); John (ID: `person:John`)

## Mark 11:33 (id: 21282)

In answering, “We don’t know,” the religious leaders of the nation admitted that they could not answer a simple question that the people had no difficulty in answering: John was sent from God. They thus forfeited the religious leadership of the nation on this question. Their hardness of heart ([3:5](https://ref.ly/Mark3:5); [7:6](https://ref.ly/Mark7:6)), their unwillingness to see ([4:12](https://ref.ly/Mark4:12)), and their deliberate refusal to accept the truth made further discussion fruitless. Their blindness and hostility toward God’s servants John the Baptist and Jesus, their desecration of the Temple, and their plot to kill Jesus all revealed why God’s judgment would come within that very generation ([13:30](https://ref.ly/Mark13:30)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 11:33

## Mark 12:1 (id: 21298)

The antecedent to them is the religious leaders of [11:27](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27) (also in [12:12](https://ref.ly/Mark12:12)). • The beginning description of the story comes from [Isa 5:1–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:1-Isa5:7). Jesus’ audience knew the book of Isaiah well and would have immediately recalled this allegory of the vineyard.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Parable (ID: `keyterm:Parable`); Thorn Hedge (ID: `realia:ThornHedge`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Watch-Tower (ID: `realia:Watch-tower`); Winepress (ID: `realia:Winepress`); Tower (ID: `realia:Tower`)

## Mark 12:1–12 (id: 21287)

The events of [11:27–12:44](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27-Mark12:44) all take place in the Temple (see [11:27](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27); [13:1](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1)). This story (literally *parable*) is intimately tied to the previous question about Jesus’ authority ([11:27–33](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27-Mark11:33)) by the introductory words, Then Jesus began teaching them. This parable is an extended reply by Jesus to the religious leaders. • Jesus intended his audience to interpret this parable in light of [Isa 5:1–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:1-Isa5:7), a similar story of someone planting a vineyard, building a lookout tower, putting a fence around the vineyard, and digging a pit for a wine vat. A similar question is asked: What do you suppose the owner of the vineyard will do? ([Mark 12:9](https://ref.ly/Mark12:9); cp. [Isa 5:4](https://ref.ly/Isa5:4)). Isaiah specifically identifies the vineyard as the people of Israel ([Isa 5:7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:7)); Jesus’ hearers and Mark’s readers would similarly have understood the present story as an allegory about the Israelites. Other details in the story are also clearly meant to be interpreted allegorically: The tenants represent the leaders of Israel; the owner represents God; the servants represent the Old Testament prophets; the beloved son represents Jesus, the Son of God; the murder of the son represents Jesus’ crucifixion; and the giving of the vineyard to others represents the judgment coming upon Israel ([Mark 11:15–17](https://ref.ly/Mark11:15-Mark11:17); [13:1–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1-Mark13:37)). The interpretation would have been more clear to Mark’s readers than to Jesus’ original audience, but the religious leaders who were Jesus’ contemporaries understood it clearly enough that they sought to kill him ([12:12](https://ref.ly/Mark12:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:1-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Parable (ID: `keyterm:Parable`); Reject (ID: `keyterm:Reject`); Beloved (ID: `keyterm:Beloved`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Written (ID: `keyterm:Scripture`); Empty (ID: `keyterm:Empty`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Thorn Hedge (ID: `realia:ThornHedge`); Cornerstone (ID: `realia:Cornerstone`); Watch-Tower (ID: `realia:Watch-tower`); Winepress (ID: `realia:Winepress`); Tower (ID: `realia:Tower`)

## Mark 12:2–5 (id: 21299)

The time of the grape harvest probably refers to the time, some four years after planting, when a new vineyard would produce its first harvest. The owner’s share of the crop would have been a quantity of grapes or wine, given in payment for the lease of the vineyard. • In the Old Testament, prophets are often referred to as servants ([Jer 7:25–26](https://ref.ly/Jer7:25-Jer7:26); [Amos 3:7](https://ref.ly/Amos3:7); [Zech 1:6](https://ref.ly/Zech1:6)); they were repeatedly mistreated and killed by Israelite leaders ([1 Kgs 18:13](https://ref.ly/1Kgs18:13); [19:10](https://ref.ly/1Kgs19:10), [14](https://ref.ly/1Kgs19:14); [2 Chr 24:20–27](https://ref.ly/2Chr24:20-2Chr24:27); [36:15–16](https://ref.ly/2Chr36:15-2Chr36:16); [Jer 26:20–23](https://ref.ly/Jer26:20-Jer26:23); [Neh 9:26](https://ref.ly/Neh9:26); see [Matt 23:34](https://ref.ly/Matt23:34), [37](https://ref.ly/Matt23:37); [1 Thes 2:15](https://ref.ly/1Thess2:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:2-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Empty (ID: `keyterm:Empty`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Mark 12:7 (id: 21314)

The reasoning of the tenants is unclear to us, although it was apparently clear to the original hearers, requiring no further explanation (cp. [Matt 21:38](https://ref.ly/Matt21:38); [Luke 20:14](https://ref.ly/Luke20:14)). Perhaps with the son’s coming, they assumed that the father had died and that the murder of the son would leave the vineyard without a living claimant. Then ownership would fall to those who had been working the vineyard for years.

## Mark 12:9 (id: 21316)

The parable concludes with two rhetorical questions (cp. [3:23](https://ref.ly/Mark3:23), [33](https://ref.ly/Mark3:33); [4:13](https://ref.ly/Mark4:13); [7:18](https://ref.ly/Mark7:18); [8:12](https://ref.ly/Mark8:12)). The first corresponds to what the “Lord of Heaven’s Armies” says in the parable of the vineyard ([Isa 5:5–7](https://ref.ly/Isa5:5-Isa5:7)). Although some interpret Jesus’ answer as meaning that God would replace the evil Jewish leaders ([Mark 11:27](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27); [12:12](https://ref.ly/Mark12:12)) with more faithful ones, Mark’s readers likely would have understood the others to whom the vineyard would be given as the Gentile church (cp. [Matt 21:43](https://ref.ly/Matt21:43); [Rom 9:25–33](https://ref.ly/Rom9:25-Rom9:33); [11:13–24](https://ref.ly/Rom11:13-Rom11:24)). Thus, Jesus’ parable connects with the coming destruction of Jerusalem ([Mark 11:15–19](https://ref.ly/Mark11:15-Mark11:19); [13:1–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1-Mark13:37); [14:58](https://ref.ly/Mark14:58); [15:29](https://ref.ly/Mark15:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Mark 12:10–11 (id: 21288)

In his second rhetorical question, Jesus used a quotation from [Ps 118:22–23](https://ref.ly/Ps118:22-Ps118:23). The rejected stone (the Son) had become the cornerstone of God’s Kingdom (cp. [Acts 4:11](https://ref.ly/Acts4:11); [1 Pet 2:4](https://ref.ly/1Pet2:4), [6–7](https://ref.ly/1Pet2:6-1Pet2:7)). The phrase is traditionally understood as the buried cornerstone that is part of the foundation, but it could be the capstone at the top of a building that marks its completion. Whichever stone is meant, it was the most important one. Despite the evil intention of the builders—the religious leaders—all they did was in accordance with God’s will ([Acts 4:28](https://ref.ly/Acts4:28)), which was wonderful to see. Those who put Jesus to death were God’s instruments in his plan for the Son of Man to give his life as a ransom for many ([Mark 10:45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:45)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:10-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Reject (ID: `keyterm:Reject`); Written (ID: `keyterm:Scripture`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Cornerstone (ID: `realia:Cornerstone`)

## Mark 12:16 (id: 21293)

The picture and title on the coin were almost certainly of Tiberius Caesar, who ruled from AD 14 to 37\. The inscription (words written on the coin) said, “Tiberius Caesar Augustus, Son of \[the] Divine Augustus” on one side and “High Priest” on the other.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tiberius (ID: `person:Tiberius`)

## Mark 12:17 (id: 21294)

give to Caesar what belongs to Caesar: Jesus’ words cannot be taken as those of an anti\-Roman zealot opposing Jewish taxation by Caesar. Jesus clearly indicates elsewhere that he was opposed to the zealot cause (see [Matt 5:9](https://ref.ly/Matt5:9), [39](https://ref.ly/Matt5:39), [41](https://ref.ly/Matt5:41); [26:52](https://ref.ly/Matt26:52)). However, Jesus’ statement cannot be understood as pro\-Roman, either, because service to God is fundamental, and God is ultimately over Caesar. Like the evil farmers in the previous parable, the religious leaders had not given God what belonged to him.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:17

## Mark 12:18 (id: 21296)

This is the only place in Mark where the Sadducees are mentioned by name, although they are included in references to the leading priests (see [8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31); [10:33](https://ref.ly/Mark10:33); [11:18](https://ref.ly/Mark11:18), [27](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27)). Sadducees did not believe in the resurrection from the dead (see [Acts 4:1–2](https://ref.ly/Acts4:1-Acts4:2); [23:8](https://ref.ly/Acts23:8)).

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Sadducees (ID: `group:Sadducee`)

## Mark 12:18–27 (id: 21295)

This is the third controversy story of the series begun in [11:27](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27). As in most New Testament references to the Sadducees*,* the setting is the Temple ([Matt 22:23–33](https://ref.ly/Matt22:23-Matt22:33) *//* [Luke 20:27–40](https://ref.ly/Luke20:27-Luke20:40); [Acts 4:1–3](https://ref.ly/Acts4:1-Acts4:3); [5:12](https://ref.ly/Acts5:12), [17](https://ref.ly/Acts5:17); [22:30–23:10](https://ref.ly/Acts22:30-Acts23:10); the exceptions are [Matt 3:7](https://ref.ly/Matt3:7); [16:1–12](https://ref.ly/Matt16:1-Matt16:12)). • The Sadducees’ question ([Mark 12:19–23](https://ref.ly/Mark12:19-Mark12:23)) was carefully crafted and based on a commandment of Moses ([Deut 25:5–6](https://ref.ly/Deut25:5-Deut25:6); see [Gen 38:6–11](https://ref.ly/Gen38:6-Gen38:11); [Ruth 4:1–22](https://ref.ly/Ruth4:1-Ruth4:22)). Since all seven men could not have the woman as wife in the resurrection, and since none of them had a special claim, the Sadducees thought that they had proven the absurdity of the doctrine of the resurrection and refuted the Pharisees and Jesus (cp. [Matt 12:41–42](https://ref.ly/Matt12:41-Matt12:42); [Luke 16:19–31](https://ref.ly/Luke16:19-Luke16:31); see also [Mark 8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31); [9:31](https://ref.ly/Mark9:31); [10:34](https://ref.ly/Mark10:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:18-27

## Mark 12:19 (id: 21297)

The Sadducees based their views only on the books of Moses. • Like the Pharisees ([12:14](https://ref.ly/Mark12:14)), they addressed Jesus as Teacher. The Sadducees were fond of debating with religious teachers (see Josephus, *Antiquities* 18\.1\.4\).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`)

## Mark 12:24–27 (id: 21300)

Jesus’ response had two elements. First, he rebuked the Sadducees’ ignorance of the Scriptures (i.e., the Old Testament), where the resurrection of the dead is referred to most clearly in the Prophets ([Isa 26:19](https://ref.ly/Isa26:19); [Ezek 37:1–14](https://ref.ly/Ezek37:1-Ezek37:14)) and the Writings ([Job 19:26](https://ref.ly/Job19:26); [Pss 16:9–11](https://ref.ly/Ps16:9-Ps16:11); [49:15](https://ref.ly/Ps49:15); [73:23–26](https://ref.ly/Ps73:23-Ps73:26); [Dan 12:2](https://ref.ly/Dan12:2)). However, the Sadducees accepted only the Torah (Genesis—Deuteronomy), so Jesus answered them from the Torah. The reference to God as the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob (see [Exod 3:6](https://ref.ly/Exod3:6), [15–16](https://ref.ly/Exod3:15-Exod3:16)) showed that God’s covenant with the patriarchs could not be broken by death. They were still alive ([Matt 8:11–12](https://ref.ly/Matt8:11-Matt8:12); [13:17](https://ref.ly/Matt13:17); [Luke 16:19–31](https://ref.ly/Luke16:19-Luke16:31); [John 8:56](https://ref.ly/John8:56)), for God is the God of the living, not the dead. God’s covenant relationship with his people extends beyond their physical death, which means that the afterlife must be a reality. • Second, Jesus challenged the Sadducees’ ignorance of God’s power. They thought that life in the resurrection would have to be an extension of mortal life. Instead, it is life raised to an entirely new level. In this new existence, there is no need for sexual reproduction because there is no more death. The intimacy of marriage is superseded by fellowship with a multitude of fellow believers and with God ([Mark 10:29–30](https://ref.ly/Mark10:29-Mark10:30); [Matt 19:28–29](https://ref.ly/Matt19:28-Matt19:29); [Luke 18:29–30](https://ref.ly/Luke18:29-Luke18:30); [Rev 21:1–4](https://ref.ly/Rev21:1-Rev21:4)). It is in this respect that resurrected believers will be like the angels in heaven. • Jesus’ argument demonstrates his wisdom before his opponents and affirms the hope of the resurrection for his followers. Jesus is “the resurrection and the life” ([John 11:25](https://ref.ly/John11:25)); he has conquered death and assured those who believe in him that they will “not perish but have eternal life” ([John 3:16](https://ref.ly/John3:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:24-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Abraham (ID: `person:Abraham`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Isaac (ID: `person:Isaac`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Written (ID: `keyterm:Scripture`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Boxthorn (ID: `flora:Boxthorn`); Brier (ID: `flora:Brier`)

## Mark 12:28 (id: 21302)

Of all the commandments: The Torah (Genesis—Deuteronomy) contains 613 separate commandments, and Jewish teachers frequently debated about which were more important than others. No command was considered unimportant, but some were recognized as more fundamental than others (see [Matt 22:40](https://ref.ly/Matt22:40)). Rabbi Hillel’s answer to this question was, “What is hateful to you, do not do to your neighbor” (*Babylonian Shabbat* 31a; see also *Tobit* 4:15\). Other suggestions included [Prov 3:6](https://ref.ly/Prov3:6); [Isa 33:15–16](https://ref.ly/Isa33:15-Isa33:16); [56:1](https://ref.ly/Isa56:1); [Amos 5:4](https://ref.ly/Amos5:4); [Mic 6:8](https://ref.ly/Mic6:8); and [Hab 2:4](https://ref.ly/Hab2:4).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** A Priest (ID: `person:GenericPriest`); A Scribe (ID: `person:Scribe`); Commandment (ID: `keyterm:Commandment`)

## Mark 12:28–34 (id: 21301)

After the three hostile challenges of [11:27–12:27](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27-Mark12:27), the reader might expect the next question to Jesus to be hostile (see [11:28](https://ref.ly/Mark11:28); [12:13](https://ref.ly/Mark12:13), [15](https://ref.ly/Mark12:15), [19–23](https://ref.ly/Mark12:19-Mark12:23)), but this was not a hostile counter. Earlier references in Mark to the teachers of religious law have portrayed them as antagonistic ([2:6–7](https://ref.ly/Mark2:6-Mark2:7), [16](https://ref.ly/Mark2:16); [3:22](https://ref.ly/Mark3:22); [7:1](https://ref.ly/Mark7:1), [5](https://ref.ly/Mark7:5); [8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31); [9:14](https://ref.ly/Mark9:14); [10:33](https://ref.ly/Mark10:33); [11:18](https://ref.ly/Mark11:18), [27](https://ref.ly/Mark11:27)), and Jesus will warn the crowds against their hypocrisy ([12:38–40](https://ref.ly/Mark12:38-Mark12:40)), but this teacher was positively inclined toward Jesus ([12:28](https://ref.ly/Mark12:28), [32](https://ref.ly/Mark12:32)) and praised him ([12:32–33](https://ref.ly/Mark12:32-Mark12:33)). Jesus described the man’s answer to his question as having understanding, and Jesus said that the man was not far from the Kingdom of God ([12:34](https://ref.ly/Mark12:34)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:28-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Commandment (ID: `keyterm:Commandment`); A Priest (ID: `person:GenericPriest`); Heart (ID: `keyterm:Heart`); A Scribe (ID: `person:Scribe`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Truth (ID: `keyterm:Truth`); Mind (ID: `keyterm:Mind.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Mark 12:29–31 (id: 21303)

Jesus’ reply combined two widely separated commands from the Torah. • The first, [Deut 6:4–5](https://ref.ly/Deut6:4-Deut6:5), was probably the best known passage in the Old Testament, for it was repeated twice daily by observant Jews. It is called the *Shema* because it begins with the word Listen (Hebrew shema‘). The first command corresponds to the first part of the Ten Commandments ([Exod 20:2–11](https://ref.ly/Exod20:2-Exod20:11)), which deals with a person’s relationship to God. • The oneness of God is foundational to Jewish and Christian monotheism and is the basis for the command to love God with all one’s heart (thinking and affection), soul (desire and feeling), mind (understanding), and strength (energy and power). • The second command is from [Lev 19:18](https://ref.ly/Lev19:18). It corresponds to the second part of the Ten Commandments ([Exod 20:12–17](https://ref.ly/Exod20:12-Exod20:17)), which concerns a person’s relationship with other people. The second command is based on the natural inclination of people to look after themselves. Its importance to the early church can be seen in its frequent repetition ([Matt 5:43–44](https://ref.ly/Matt5:43-Matt5:44); [19:19](https://ref.ly/Matt19:19); [25:31–46](https://ref.ly/Matt25:31-Matt25:46); [Rom 13:8–10](https://ref.ly/Rom13:8-Rom13:10); [Gal 5:14](https://ref.ly/Gal5:14); [Jas 2:8](https://ref.ly/Jas2:8); *Didache* 1\.2; 2\.7\). The two commands are not independent, but are intimately associated as one commandment. Their integration precludes religious mysticism that addresses only a person’s relationship with God or humanism that addresses only human relationships.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:29-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Heart (ID: `keyterm:Heart`); Commandment (ID: `keyterm:Commandment`); Mind (ID: `keyterm:Mind.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`)

The scribe’s affirmation adds emphasis to the importance of these two commands for authentic devotion.

## Mark 12:34 (id: 21305)

The account ends with Jesus’ commending the teacher of religious law for recognizing that this twofold command was more important than burnt offerings and sacrifices (see [Hos 6:6](https://ref.ly/Hos6:6)). We are left uncertain as to the fate of this man. He was not far from the Kingdom of God, but did he enter it? Mark might have intentionally left this question unanswered so that each reader would wrestle with the question, *Have I entered the Kingdom of God?*

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 12:35–37 (id: 21306)

After having answered various questions, mostly from opponents, Jesus now asked a question (see [8:27](https://ref.ly/Mark8:27); [Matt 17:25](https://ref.ly/Matt17:25); [21:31](https://ref.ly/Matt21:31); [Luke 10:36](https://ref.ly/Luke10:36)). The Messiah was considered the son of David because of such passages as [Isa 9:2–7](https://ref.ly/Isa9:2-Isa9:7); [11:1–5](https://ref.ly/Isa11:1-Isa11:5); [Jer 23:5–6](https://ref.ly/Jer23:5-Jer23:6); [33:15–16](https://ref.ly/Jer33:15-Jer33:16); [Ezek 34:23–24](https://ref.ly/Ezek34:23-Ezek34:24); [37:24–28](https://ref.ly/Ezek37:24-Ezek37:28). With his question, Jesus did not deny that the Messiah was a descendant of David ([Mark 10:47–48](https://ref.ly/Mark10:47-Mark10:48); [11:10](https://ref.ly/Mark11:10); see [Rom 1:3–4](https://ref.ly/Rom1:3-Rom1:4)), but he demonstrated that this description, while correct, is inadequate. The Messiah is far more!

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:35-37

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** David (ID: `person:David`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Mark 12:36 (id: 21307)

[Psalm 110:1](https://ref.ly/Ps110:1) is the Old Testament verse most quoted in the New Testament. Since David authored the psalm, the statement “The Lord \[God] said to my \[David’s] Lord \[the Messiah]” indicates that the Messiah is David’s Lord. Thus, the Messiah could not simply be David’s son. He is the Son of God who sits at God’s right hand ([Mark 14:62](https://ref.ly/Mark14:62)) and will come to judge the world ([8:38](https://ref.ly/Mark8:38); [13:24–27](https://ref.ly/Mark13:24-Mark13:27), [32–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32-Mark13:37)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:36-37

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); David (ID: `person:David`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 12:38–39 (id: 21308)

teachers of religious law: See [1:22](https://ref.ly/Mark1:22). The warning here is similar to the one against the Pharisees and Herod in [8:15](https://ref.ly/Mark8:15). The scribes broke the two greatest commands ([12:30–31](https://ref.ly/Mark12:30-Mark12:31)). They broke the first command by failing to love God and failing to reserve for him alone the reverence and adoration they sought for themselves. • Flowing robes were probably the garments worn by religious men; respectful greetings perhaps meant being called “Rabbi” (see [Matt 23:7](https://ref.ly/Matt23:7)). The seats of honor were in the front of the synagogue facing the congregation.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:38-39

## Mark 12:40 (id: 21309)

The teachers of religious law broke the second command by failing to love their neighbors, as was seen in their cheating widows of their property. They clearly were cheating the most vulnerable and needy people despite God’s concern for widows ([Deut 14:29](https://ref.ly/Deut14:29); [Pss 68:5](https://ref.ly/Ps68:5); [146:9](https://ref.ly/Ps146:9); [Isa 1:17](https://ref.ly/Isa1:17); [Jer 7:6](https://ref.ly/Jer7:6); [49:11](https://ref.ly/Jer49:11)) and his condemnation of those who mistreat them ([Isa 1:23](https://ref.ly/Isa1:23); [Ezek 22:7](https://ref.ly/Ezek22:7); [Zech 7:10](https://ref.ly/Zech7:10); [Mal 3:5](https://ref.ly/Mal3:5)). The scribes wore a hypocritical cloak of false piety (see [Matt 6:5–6](https://ref.ly/Matt6:5-Matt6:6); [Luke 18:11–12](https://ref.ly/Luke18:11-Luke18:12)). • they will be more severely punished: In the final judgment ([Mark 9:42–48](https://ref.ly/Mark9:42-Mark9:48); see [Luke 14:11](https://ref.ly/Luke14:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:40

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judgment (ID: `keyterm:Judgment`); Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`)

## Mark 12:41–42 (id: 21310)

The word translated collection box can refer to a building within the Temple compound in which Temple money was stored, but here it refers to one of thirteen money chests into which gifts were placed. As Jesus watched, many rich people put in large sums. Then he noticed a poor widow put in two small coins. (The use of the Roman term *quadrans* supports the view that Mark wrote his Gospel for the church in Rome; see Mark Book Introduction, “Audience.”) These two coins were worth one sixty\-fourth of a *denarius*, a normal day’s pay ([Matt 20:1–2](https://ref.ly/Matt20:1-Matt20:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:41-42

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Quadrans (ID: `realia:Quadrans`); Lepton (ID: `realia:Lepton`); Treasury (ID: `realia:Treasury`)

## Mark 12:41–44 (id: 21311)

This account of a poor widow highlights the contrast between the falsely pious religious leaders ([12:38–40](https://ref.ly/Mark12:38-Mark12:40)) and those who truly love God. The emphasis of the passage falls on Jesus’ pronouncement, I tell you the truth, in which the widow is described as having given more than the rich.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:41-44

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Treasury (ID: `realia:Treasury`); Quadrans (ID: `realia:Quadrans`); Surely! (ID: `keyterm:Surely`); Daily Life (ID: `keyterm:DailyLife`); Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Lepton (ID: `realia:Lepton`)

## Mark 12:43–44 (id: 21312)

Jesus called his disciples to hear his teaching (see [3:23](https://ref.ly/Mark3:23); [6:7](https://ref.ly/Mark6:7); [8:1](https://ref.ly/Mark8:1); [10:42](https://ref.ly/Mark10:42); see also [7:14](https://ref.ly/Mark7:14); [8:34](https://ref.ly/Mark8:34)). Jesus’ pronouncement in [12:43](https://ref.ly/Mark12:43) revealed a radical difference between his thinking and that of the world. No one would have named a building after the widow for her gift of two small coins, but Jesus and his Father look at a person’s heart ([1 Sam 16:7](https://ref.ly/1Sam16:7)). The widow was doing exactly what Jesus told the rich young ruler to do ([Mark 10:21](https://ref.ly/Mark10:21)) and what he taught his disciples ([1:18](https://ref.ly/Mark1:18), [20](https://ref.ly/Mark1:20); [8:34–37](https://ref.ly/Mark8:34-Mark8:37); [10:28–29](https://ref.ly/Mark10:28-Mark10:29)). Like the woman of [14:3–9](https://ref.ly/Mark14:3-Mark14:9), the poor widow loved God with all her heart, soul, mind, and strength ([12:30](https://ref.ly/Mark12:30)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 12:43-44

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Daily Life (ID: `keyterm:DailyLife`); Surely! (ID: `keyterm:Surely`); Treasury (ID: `realia:Treasury`)

## Mark 13:1 (id: 21327)

Leaving the Temple, Jesus was heading to the Mount of Olives ([13:3](https://ref.ly/Mark13:3)). • look at these magnificent buildings! The sight of the Temple would have been awe\-inspiring. It was the largest temple complex in the world, with immense stones. One stone that has been uncovered in the western wall is estimated to weigh 600 tons. With its white stones, gold trim, and the gold\-covered roof, the Temple complex looked like a snow\-covered mountain; in the sun it was a blinding sight (Josephus, *War* 5\.5\.6\). The Talmud says that “He who has not seen the temple in its full construction has never seen a glorious building in his life” (*Babylonian Sukkah* 51b).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); A Group of Disciples (ID: `group:GenericDisciples`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Mark 13:2 (id: 21335)

Jesus’ reply to the disciples was shocking. This glorious and massive Temple complex, a symbol of strength and permanence and God’s favor for the Jews, would be totally, irrevocably destroyed. • Not one stone will be left on top of another! Jesus’ prediction emphasized the total devastation that would result from the Roman army’s systematic attack on the entire Temple complex (Josephus, *Antiquities* 6\.9\.1; 7\.1\.1\.). To say that Jesus’ prediction was not fulfilled because some of the foundation stones still stand is to misunderstand the language of prophecy. One does not expect a prophet to say that 97\.9% of these stones will be removed! Anyone in the first century who visited Jerusalem after AD 70 would have acknowledged that Jesus’ prediction had been fulfilled.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`)

## Mark 13:3–4 (id: 21336)

Andrew appeared here with the trio, Peter*,* James*,* and John ([5:37](https://ref.ly/Mark5:37); [9:2](https://ref.ly/Mark9:2); [14:33](https://ref.ly/Mark14:33)), completing the two sets of brothers ([1:16–20](https://ref.ly/Mark1:16-Mark1:20); [3:16–18](https://ref.ly/Mark3:16-Mark3:18)). • Jesus’ prediction elicited two questions from the disciples. Although some scholars argue that the second question goes beyond the first in looking to the coming of the Son of Man at the end of the age, it is best to interpret these two questions as focusing on the time and the sign associated with the destruction of Jerusalem and the Temple (cp. [13:29–30](https://ref.ly/Mark13:29-Mark13:30); [Luke 21:7](https://ref.ly/Luke21:7)). These questions follow naturally from Jesus’ prediction in [Mark 13:2](https://ref.ly/Mark13:2). The desire to know the sign reflected the disciples’ desire to be forewarned and prepared for all . . . these things.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:3-4
