## Mark 13:5–6 (id: 21345)

Jesus warned his followers not to be misled by the many false messiahs who would come claiming, ‘I am the Messiah’ and would deceive many into following them. These false claimants would profess to be the long\-awaited Jewish Messiah (not Jesus per se) or to speak on the Messiah’s behalf. Such claimants included Theudas the Galilean ([Acts 5:36](https://ref.ly/Acts5:36)), Simon the son of Gioras, and John of Gischala, who deceived many in the AD 60s.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:5-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`)

## Mark 13:5–23 (id: 21344)

This section is often divided into two parts, [13:5–13](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:13) and [13:14–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14-Mark13:23). The first part is often interpreted as describing the destruction of Jerusalem, which occurred in AD 70, while the second part is taken to describe the coming of the Son of Man in the future. It is best, however, to interpret all of [13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23) as describing events surrounding the destruction of Jerusalem in AD 70 because: (1\)[13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23) is Jesus’ answer to the two questions ([13:4](https://ref.ly/Mark13:4)) that deal with the destruction of Jerusalem ([13:2](https://ref.ly/Mark13:2)); (2\) The commands to flee Judea ([13:14–16](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14-Mark13:16)), the woe announced upon pregnant and nursing women ([13:17](https://ref.ly/Mark13:17)), and the prayer that it not take place in winter ([13:18](https://ref.ly/Mark13:18)) make sense if they refer to the events of AD 70 but not if they refer to the future return of Christ; (3\) Three warnings in this passage ([13:5](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5), [9](https://ref.ly/Mark13:9), [23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:23)) tie this passage together and indicate that [13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23) should be understood as a unit. • The subdivisions of this section are arranged as a chiasm (X\-pattern): A: Deceivers claim to be the Messiah ([13:5–6](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:6)). B: There are reports of fighting and natural disasters ([13:7–8](https://ref.ly/Mark13:7-Mark13:8)). C: There is persecution of believers ([13:9–13](https://ref.ly/Mark13:9-Mark13:13)). B': The fighting in Judea and resulting tribulation begin ([13:14–20](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14-Mark13:20)). A': Deceivers claim to be the Messiah ([13:21–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:21-Mark13:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:5-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Chosen (ID: `keyterm:Chosen`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); Portent (ID: `keyterm:Portent`); Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); Judea (ID: `place:Judea`); Symbol (ID: `keyterm:Example`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Good News (ID: `keyterm:GoodNews`); Abomination of Desolation (ID: `keyterm:AbominationOfDesolation`); Body (ID: `keyterm:Body.3`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Disaster (ID: `keyterm:Disaster`); Proclaim (ID: `keyterm:Proclaim`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`); Housetop (ID: `realia:Housetop`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`)

## Mark 13:7 (id: 21346)

The Greek word translated must is also used in [8:31](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31). In both instances, God’s sovereignty over events is emphasized. • but the end won’t follow immediately: These things would occur, and just as birth pains are followed by childbirth, God’s judgment on Jerusalem would follow. However, these events did not indicate that it was going to happen right away.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:7


## Mark 13:9 (id: 21347)

The second watch out! warns of persecution that was to come upon Christians (see [6:11](https://ref.ly/Mark6:11); [8:34–38](https://ref.ly/Mark8:34-Mark8:38); [10:30](https://ref.ly/Mark10:30); see [4:17](https://ref.ly/Mark4:17)). Jewish Christians would be brought before local councils of Jewish leaders, who had authority over Jewish communities. We read of such a council in [Matt 10:17](https://ref.ly/Matt10:17) and probably in [Matt 5:22](https://ref.ly/Matt5:22) (see also [Acts 4:1–22](https://ref.ly/Acts4:1-Acts4:22)). • beaten in the synagogues: Paul’s beatings in [2 Cor 11:24–25](https://ref.ly/2Cor11:24-2Cor11:25) were probably inflicted in the local synagogue. • The followers of Jesus would also be susceptible to trial before governors and kings (see [Acts 23:24](https://ref.ly/Acts23:24); [24:10–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:10-Acts24:27); [25:1–26:32](https://ref.ly/Acts25:1-Acts26:32)). Such trials were due to their being followers of Jesus ([Mark 13:9](https://ref.ly/Mark13:9), [13](https://ref.ly/Mark13:13)), not on account of real wrongdoing (see [1 Pet 4:14–16](https://ref.ly/1Pet4:14-1Pet4:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`)

## Mark 13:10 (id: 21318)

Such trials ([13:9](https://ref.ly/Mark13:9), [11](https://ref.ly/Mark13:11)) would be a means through which the Good News would be preached to all nations. This would all take place first—i.e., before the destruction of Jerusalem. For Paul’s understanding of how the Good News had already been preached to every nation in his day, see [Rom 16:26](https://ref.ly/Rom16:26); [Col 1:6](https://ref.ly/Col1:6), [23](https://ref.ly/Col1:23) (see also [Rom 1:5](https://ref.ly/Rom1:5), [8](https://ref.ly/Rom1:8); [10:18](https://ref.ly/Rom10:18); [15:19](https://ref.ly/Rom15:19), [23](https://ref.ly/Rom15:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Good News (ID: `keyterm:GoodNews`); Proclaim (ID: `keyterm:Proclaim`)

## Mark 13:11 (id: 21319)

A word of encouragement follows the warning of [13:9](https://ref.ly/Mark13:9). Jesus’ followers need not fear what they should say in these circumstances. The early followers of Jesus were generally uneducated and without political influence (see [Acts 4:10–17](https://ref.ly/Acts4:10-Acts4:17); [1 Cor 1:26](https://ref.ly/1Cor1:26)), so this assurance would have comforted them. Believers are not prohibited from thinking about what they will say, but they need not worry about it.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`)

## Mark 13:12–13 (id: 21320)

There would be no single group, not even their own families, to whom persecuted Christians could automatically flee or turn for help (see [Matt 10:35–36](https://ref.ly/Matt10:35-Matt10:36); [Luke 12:53](https://ref.ly/Luke12:53)). They would be universally hated because of their allegiance to Jesus (because you are my followers). Those who remain faithful to death (to the end) will be saved from eternal punishment (see also [Mark 8:35](https://ref.ly/Mark8:35); [Rev 2:7](https://ref.ly/Rev2:7), [10](https://ref.ly/Rev2:10), [17](https://ref.ly/Rev2:17), [26–28](https://ref.ly/Rev2:26-Rev2:28); [3:5](https://ref.ly/Rev3:5), [12](https://ref.ly/Rev3:12), [21](https://ref.ly/Rev3:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:12-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)

## Mark 13:14 (id: 21322)

* *When you see the abomination of desolation*: Mark did not explain what this object would be, but Jewish audiences in the first century were familiar with the term. The prophet Daniel had foretold that such an object would stand in the temple in Jerusalem ([Daniel 9:27](https://ref.ly/Dan9:27); [11:31](https://ref.ly/Dan11:31)). Many Jews understood the events in Jerusalem from 167 to 164 BC, during the time of Antiochus IV Epiphanes, to be a fulfillment of that prophecy.

    The deuterocanonical book of 1 Maccabees(a book accepted as Scripture by some Christian traditions)*,* was written around 100 BC. It tells about the rule of Antiochus. It says that Antiochus and his followers set up “sacrilegious object causing desecration on top of the altar for burnt offerings,” *1 Maccabees* 1:54, 59\).

* *standing where it should not be*: Given the historical context and references to Jerusalem and Judea, this phrase clearly refers to something inappropriate happening in the temple in Jerusalem ([Mark 13:2](https://ref.ly/Mark13:2), [4](https://ref.ly/Mark13:4),[14](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14)).
* *(let the reader understand)*: Mark urged his readers to focus on this sign, indicating it required careful thought and understanding. Mark wanted his audience to watch for a sign similar to what happened during the time of Antiochus Epiphanes.
* Although the phrase *the abomination of desolation* is grammatically neuter, the word *standing* is grammatically masculine. This may suggest a person behind the act, but it is not certain. The phrase can still refer to a thing or event.

    Scholars suggest several possible meanings for the *abomination of desolation*. These include:

    1. The emperor Caligula. In AD 39–40, he tried to place a statue of himself in the temple (Josephus, *Antiquities* 12\.8\.2–3\).
        2. Pontius Pilate, governor of Judea from AD 26–36\. He brought Roman soldiers into Judea carrying military flags that Jews saw as idolatrous (Josephus, *War* 2\.9\.2–3\).
        3. The Zealots, a Jewish revolutionary group. Around AD 69–70, they committed violent acts in the temple, chose an unqualified high priest, "entered the sanctuary with polluted feet" (Josephus, *War* 4\.3\.4–8\).
        4. The Roman general Titus. After he conquered Jerusalem in AD 70, he forced entry into the temple (Josephus, *War* 6\.4\.7\).
        5. Titus' soldiers. They set up their military flags in the temple, offered sacrifices to them, and declared Titus emperor (Josephus, *War* 6\.6\.1\).
        6. The destruction of the temple itself in AD 70\.
        7. A future event. Some connect this to the coming of the antichrist (a future opponent of God; see [2 Thessalonians 2:3–4](https://ref.ly/2Thess2:3-2Thess2:4)).
        The context and source of this expression help us rule out some of these views. In Daniel and 1 Maccabees, the phrase refers to the Temple, its altar, and its sacrifices. In [Mark 13:14–20](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14-Mark13:20), it is a sign that tells people to flee Judea. This sign must happen while there is still time to escape (before the Roman army surrounds Jerusalem).

    Explanations 1 and 2 are too early. They would not have been a clear sign to flee Judea. They also did not actually defile the temple, and Christians did not leave Jerusalem at that time.

    Explanations 4–6 are too late. They happened after Titus entered Jerusalem. By then, there was no time left to escape.

    Explanation 7 refers to a future event. It does not match the destruction of Jerusalem in AD 70, which is the focus of [13:14–20](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14-Mark13:20). Also, the coming of the antichrist would not be limited to Judea.

    Explanation 3 fits best. It took place in AD 69–70, shortly before Titus surrounded Jerusalem. This timing would have allowed a short time for Christians to leave the city. It also involved actions that defiled the holy place in the temple.

    This view also helps distinguish between the sign and what happens next. The sign is the *abomination of desolation*, and what happens next is the great suffering and the destruction of Jerusalem and the temple.

* *then*: At the appearance of the *abomination*, those in Judea were to *flee to the mountains*. The early church historian Eusebius says that a prophetic message (a message from God) was given to the church in Jerusalem. This message caused them to leave the city before it was destroyed (Eusebius, *Church History* 3\.5\.3\).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Abomination of Desolation (ID: `keyterm:AbominationOfDesolation`); Judea (ID: `place:Judea`)

## Mark 13:14–20 (id: 21321)

Jesus now gave the sign requested in [13:4](https://ref.ly/Mark13:4) and instructed his followers how to respond when they saw it. In [13:5–13](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:13), they were told not to be alarmed. Here they are told that those in Judea must flee to the hills.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:14-20

## Mark 13:15–16 (id: 21323)

A person relaxing on the roof of their Judean home should not even pack after seeing this sign, but come down and flee. Likewise, a person out in the field should not return home to retrieve his cloak. Believers were to flee from the approaching Roman army as soon as they saw the sign of [13:14](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14). 

Their movement was usually cautious, methodical (careful and step\-by\-step), and relentless (steady and not stopping). But Jesus warned people not to wait and see how things would develop.

The suffering of the weakest (*pregnant and nursing mothers*) shows that the coming disaster would be severe. On that day, the joy of being a mother would be accompanied by terrible trouble (see [Luke 1:25](https://ref.ly/Luke1:25), [57–59](https://ref.ly/Luke1:57-Luke1:59)).

In winter, the wadis (canyon\-like riverbeds) are flooded, travel is more difficult, and survival is harder.

## Mark 13:19 (id: 21326)

greater anguish in those days than at any time since God created the world: Such hyperbole is common in Semitic expression; it heightens the terror of that horrible time and should not be taken as an exact statistical analysis of how this suffering ranks alongside other disasters. • And it will never be so great again: The events of [13:14–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14-Mark13:23) would not bring history to an end; history would continue after the destruction of the Temple in AD 70\.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 13:20 (id: 21328)

God’s shortening of his timetable for the days of calamity is referred to frequently in intertestamental Jewish literature (e.g., *2 Esdras* 2:13; *2 Baruch* 20:1\). This statement emphasizes the horrors of this tribulation experienced by God’s people, but also God’s mercy in shortening this time. • not a single person will survive: The whole population of Judea might have been destroyed if the days of anguish had been longer. • chosen ones (literally *elect*): Followers of Jesus.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Body (ID: `keyterm:Body.3`); Chosen (ID: `keyterm:Chosen`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 13:21–23 (id: 21329)

The larger section ([13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23)) concludes with another warning about messianic pretenders. Here the pretenders are associated with the events of AD 70, whereas in [13:5–6](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:6) they were associated with the normal course of events. Along with false messiahs, prophets would appear and perform miraculous signs and wonders ([13:22](https://ref.ly/Mark13:22)), hoping to deceive not only the Jews of Judea and Jerusalem but even the Christians (God’s chosen ones). Jesus warned his followers not to believe such reports. When the Messiah comes from heaven ([13:26](https://ref.ly/Mark13:26)), everyone will see and know it (see [Rev 1:7](https://ref.ly/Rev1:7)). • Watch out! This warning unifies the section ([Mark 13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23)) and brings it to a close. Jesus’ teaching in this section was to warn his followers in Judea and Jerusalem not to be misled by false messianic hopes and claims. Many Jews succumbed to such claims in the late AD 60s, and Josephus (*Antiquities* 17\.10\.8\) describes the great harm done by these pretenders, who encouraged the Jewish people to resist the Romans. Nothing should distract Christians from fleeing Judea and Jerusalem when they see the sacrilegious object that causes desecration taking place. • For Mark’s readers in Rome, Jesus’ message had a different application. Mark wanted his readers to watch out for those who promised timetables for prophecy to be fulfilled. They could not know the time ([13:32](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32)), and a frenzy about the second coming of Christ was forbidden, but they were to be alert ([13:33–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:33-Mark13:37)) and prepare themselves for persecution ([13:9–13](https://ref.ly/Mark13:9-Mark13:13); see [8:34–38](https://ref.ly/Mark8:34-Mark8:38)) according to Jesus’ words of encouragement ([13:11](https://ref.ly/Mark13:11), [13](https://ref.ly/Mark13:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:21-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Symbol (ID: `keyterm:Example`); Chosen (ID: `keyterm:Chosen`); Portent (ID: `keyterm:Portent`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`)

## Mark 13:24–25 (id: 21330)

Some of the language used in the New Testament to describe Jesus’ second coming, such as the “trumpet call of God” ([1 Thes 4:16](https://ref.ly/1Thess4:16)), appears to be metaphorical, and in the Old Testament, cosmic language is frequently used to describe historical events metaphorically (see [Isa 11:1–9](https://ref.ly/Isa11:1-Isa11:9); [13:9–11](https://ref.ly/Isa13:9-Isa13:11); [Jer 4:23–28](https://ref.ly/Jer4:23-Jer4:28); [Ezek 32:1–16](https://ref.ly/Ezek32:1-Ezek32:16)). So it is possible that this imagery could refer to a past event, such as the destruction of Jerusalem, if that is the meaning of [Mark 13:24–27](https://ref.ly/Mark13:24-Mark13:27). However, the New Testament writers clearly understood the coming of the Son of Man to be the visible and personal return of Jesus in the future (see [Acts 1:9–11](https://ref.ly/Acts1:9-Acts1:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:24-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`)

## Mark 13:24–27 (id: 21331)

At that time, after the anguish of those days: Many scholars argue that the cosmic signs of [13:24–25](https://ref.ly/Mark13:24-Mark13:25), the coming of the Son of Man in [13:26](https://ref.ly/Mark13:26), and the gathering of the chosen ones from throughout the world in [13:27](https://ref.ly/Mark13:27) are metaphorical ways of referring to the destruction of Jerusalem in AD 70 and to the vindication of the Son of Man by that event. The traditional interpretation, though, is that, whereas the former material refers to the destruction of Jerusalem, this passage refers to the coming of the Son of Man (the *parousia*) that will occur at the end of history. This interpretation fits better for several reasons: (1\) After the anguish of those days means after the destruction of Jerusalem, not during it, and at that time (literally *in those days*) could occur at any time after the events of [13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23); (2\) several words used in [13:26](https://ref.ly/Mark13:26) are used elsewhere to describe the coming of the Son of Man: glory ([8:38](https://ref.ly/Mark8:38)), power and clouds ([14:62](https://ref.ly/Mark14:62)); and (3\) in light of the early church’s longing and praying for the return of the Lord Jesus ([1 Cor 16:22](https://ref.ly/1Cor16:22); [Rev 22:20](https://ref.ly/Rev22:20)), Mark’s readers would have interpreted [Mark 13:26](https://ref.ly/Mark13:26) as the second coming of Jesus, which will bring history as we know it to a close. The prophets, Jesus, and the Gospel writers described this event as though seen through a telescope, and the distance between events is unclear; no one knows the time for this event except God himself ([13:32](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32)). The events of [13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23) and [13:24–27](https://ref.ly/Mark13:24-Mark13:27) are part of the same great, divine act that includes the coming of the Son of Man, his ministry, death, and resurrection, the judgment of Jerusalem in AD 70, and the Son of Man’s final coming in glory.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:24-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Chosen (ID: `keyterm:Chosen`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`)

## Mark 13:27 (id: 21332)

The Son of Man, at his coming, will gather his chosen people—those who believe in him and follow him—from all over the world (see [Zech 2:6](https://ref.ly/Zech2:6)) and from the farthest ends of the earth and heaven (see [Deut 13:7](https://ref.ly/Deut13:7); [30:4](https://ref.ly/Deut30:4); [Isa 42:10](https://ref.ly/Isa42:10); [62:11](https://ref.ly/Isa62:11)). This hope is expressed frequently in the Old Testament (e.g., [Ps 107:2–3](https://ref.ly/Ps107:2-Ps107:3); [Isa 11:11–16](https://ref.ly/Isa11:11-Isa11:16); [27:12–13](https://ref.ly/Isa27:12-Isa27:13); [43:5–13](https://ref.ly/Isa43:5-Isa43:13); [49:12](https://ref.ly/Isa49:12); [60:1–9](https://ref.ly/Isa60:1-Isa60:9); [Jer 31:10](https://ref.ly/Jer31:10)). For Mark’s readers, this passage would have provided encouragement to endure and remain faithful despite the persecutions foretold in [Mark 13:9–13](https://ref.ly/Mark13:9-Mark13:13). The Son of Man’s coming will also bring judgment on the unrighteous ([8:38](https://ref.ly/Mark8:38); [13:32–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32-Mark13:37); [Matt 13:41–43](https://ref.ly/Matt13:41-Matt13:43); [24:36–51](https://ref.ly/Matt24:36-Matt24:51); [25:1–12](https://ref.ly/Matt25:1-Matt25:12), [31–46](https://ref.ly/Matt25:31-Matt25:46)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Chosen (ID: `keyterm:Chosen`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`)

It is closely tied to [13:4–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:4-Mark13:23) by the words all these things ([13:4](https://ref.ly/Mark13:4)) and the expression when you see ([13:29](https://ref.ly/Mark13:29); cp. ## Mark 13:29 (id: 21334)

his return (literally *he/it is near*): The Greek has no explicit subject, so the choice between “he is near” or “it is near” depends on whether [13:29–30](https://ref.ly/Mark13:29-Mark13:30) refers to [13:4–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:4-Mark13:23) or to [13:24–27](https://ref.ly/Mark13:24-Mark13:27). The NLT text understands it as referring to the return of the Son of Man. Because words in [13:4](https://ref.ly/Mark13:4) are echoed in [13:29](https://ref.ly/Mark13:29) and [30](https://ref.ly/Mark13:30) (see study note on 13:28–31), others interpret it as referring to the destruction of Jerusalem. Just as the sprouting of the fig tree is a herald of summer, the desolating sacrilege ([13:14](https://ref.ly/Mark13:14)) would be a harbinger of Jerusalem’s destruction.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:29

## Mark 13:30 (id: 21337)

This generation was to witness the fulfillment of these events. That generation did witness the destruction of Jerusalem. Those, however, who hold that all these things refers to the coming of the Son of Man have to interpret this generation as meaning something other than “people alive at this time.” It could refer to the continued existence of the Jewish people, the whole human race, the Christian community, or the last generation of the end time.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Surely! (ID: `keyterm:Surely`)

## Mark 13:31 (id: 21338)

Jesus personally guaranteed what he had said. My words include specifically what Jesus said in the whole discourse of [Mark 13:1–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1-Mark13:37). Mark’s readers would have understood this as guaranteeing the truth of all Jesus’ teachings known to them ([Luke 1:2](https://ref.ly/Luke1:2)). Like the Old Testament Scriptures (see [Isa 40:8](https://ref.ly/Isa40:8)), Jesus’ words are eternal. His teachings are more abiding than the fundamental elements of creation. The basic elements of creation will pass away (see also [Ps 102:25–26](https://ref.ly/Ps102:25-Ps102:26); [Isa 40:6–8](https://ref.ly/Isa40:6-Isa40:8); [51:6](https://ref.ly/Isa51:6); [Matt 5:18](https://ref.ly/Matt5:18); [Luke 16:17](https://ref.ly/Luke16:17); [2 Pet 3:7](https://ref.ly/2Pet3:7), [10](https://ref.ly/2Pet3:10); [Rev 20:11](https://ref.ly/Rev20:11)), but Jesus’ words will never disappear.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`)

## Mark 13:32 (id: 21340)

The statement no one knows the day or hour (cp. [Acts 1:7](https://ref.ly/Acts1:7)) introduces the warning to be watchful ([Mark 13:33–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:33-Mark13:37)) and discourages speculation. • Jesus refers to himself as the Son*,* higher than the angels in an ascending hierarchy that begins with no one and ends with only the Father. The authenticity of this saying is assured by the limitation it places on the Son’s knowledge, something the early church would not likely have imagined—in the apocryphal gospels (about AD 150–300\), the tendency is to exalt and magnify Jesus’ divine attributes (e.g., see the *Infancy Gospel of Thomas*). In the miracle of the incarnation, Jesus experienced limitation ([Mark 10:40](https://ref.ly/Mark10:40); [13:32](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32)). If the Son himself did not know the day or hour, Christians should refrain from seeking such knowledge for themselves.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 13:32–37 (id: 21339)

Just as [13:28–31](https://ref.ly/Mark13:28-Mark13:31) seems to pick up the theme of [13:5–23](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5-Mark13:23) in speaking of the destruction of Jerusalem, [13:32–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32-Mark13:37) apparently picks up the theme of [13:24–27](https://ref.ly/Mark13:24-Mark13:27) and speaks of the coming of the Son of Man. An introductory warning against speculations concerning the end time ([13:32](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32)) is followed by a warning to be on guard because one cannot know the time of the end ([13:33](https://ref.ly/Mark13:33)). A story illustrates the need to be ready for the Lord’s return ([13:34](https://ref.ly/Mark13:34)), and its application repeats the need to watch lest they be found unprepared ([13:35–36](https://ref.ly/Mark13:35-Mark13:36)); the concluding warning is also for watchfulness ([13:37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:37)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:32-37

## Mark 13:33–37 (id: 21341)

These verses repeat a basic warning: *Be on your guard and stay alert!* … *Keep watch!* The repetition shows that they needed to stay alert (be watchful and ready).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:33-37

## Mark 13:34–35 (id: 21342)

The point of the story is not that the return of the Lord is uncertain or unexpected (see [Matt 24:45–51](https://ref.ly/Matt24:45-Matt24:51); [25:1–30](https://ref.ly/Matt25:1-Matt25:30); [Luke 12:36–38](https://ref.ly/Luke12:36-Luke12:38); [19:12–27](https://ref.ly/Luke19:12-Luke19:27)), but that there will be no sign or warning of his coming. The parable is allegorical in at least two respects, with the master (Greek kurios) understood as the Lord \[kurios] Jesus Christ and the slaves or household servants as the Christian community. But to allegorize the gatekeeper as the apostles or Peter was a post–New Testament development. • in the evening, at midnight, before dawn (literally *the cock’s crowing*), or at daybreak: The four periods of the night correspond to the Roman division of the night into four watches of three hours each and simply mean that the master can return at any time during the night.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:34-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Authority (ID: `keyterm:Authority.2`); To Command (ID: `keyterm:Command`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`)

## Mark 13:37 (id: 21343)

The passage, originally addressed to the disciples (you, see also [13:5](https://ref.ly/Mark13:5)), is also directed to all readers of Mark’s Gospel (everyone). • Watch for him! Although [13:32–37](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32-Mark13:37) warns about being ready, the early church experienced this vigilance as a joyous anticipation of “that wonderful day when the glory of our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ, will be revealed” ([Titus 2:13](https://ref.ly/Titus2:13)). Even Greek Christians in the first century repeated the Aramaic prayer, *Marana tha* (“Come, Lord,” see [1 Cor 16:22](https://ref.ly/1Cor16:22); [Rev 22:20](https://ref.ly/Rev22:20)) and today, we still “eagerly look forward to his appearing” ([2 Tim 4:8](https://ref.ly/2Tim4:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 13:37

## Mark 14:3–9 (id: 21364)

The story of Jesus’ being anointed by a woman in Bethany ([14:3–9](https://ref.ly/Mark14:3-Mark14:9)) sets the scene for events to follow. Luke’s account ([Luke 7:36–50](https://ref.ly/Luke7:36-Luke7:50)) is significantly different and might be a different event. This incident took place in Bethany*,* two miles east of Jerusalem on the lower, eastern slope of the Mount of Olives, where Jesus apparently stayed when he was in Judea ([Mark 11:1](https://ref.ly/Mark11:1), [11–12](https://ref.ly/Mark11:11-Mark11:12)). The home belonged to Simon*,* a former leper (lepers were isolated from society; perhaps he had been healed by Jesus; see [1:40–45](https://ref.ly/Mark1:40-Mark1:45)). • eating: Or *reclining.* The meal was a banquet, as indicated by their reclining. A woman (see [John 12:3](https://ref.ly/John12:3)) broke the neck of a sealed, alabaster jar containing expensive perfume (pure nard) and poured it all on Jesus’ head (see [Exod 29:4–7](https://ref.ly/Exod29:4-Exod29:7); [2 Kgs 9:1–6](https://ref.ly/2Kgs9:1-2Kgs9:6)).

## Mark 14:13–15 (id: 21353)

Jesus’ instructions to the disciples are similar to those of [11:2–6](https://ref.ly/Mark11:2-Mark11:6), suggesting that Jesus had prearranged a place for eating the Passover and Last Supper with his disciples. Jesus’ careful preparations for this meal emphasize its importance. The two disciples were to prepare the Passover meal*,* which included the lamb (which had to be slaughtered, skinned, cleaned, and roasted over an open fire), unleavened bread, a bowl of salt water, bitter herbs, and a bowl of a fruit puree, or kharosheth. Enough wine mixed with water was needed so that Jesus and the disciples could each drink four cups to celebrate God’s fourfold blessing ([Exod 6:6–7](https://ref.ly/Exod6:6-Exod6:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:13-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Upstairs Room (ID: `realia:UpstairsRoom`); Guest Room (ID: `realia:GuestRoom`); Passover (ID: `keyterm:Passover`); Clay Jar (ID: `realia:ClayJar`)

## Mark 14:17 (id: 21354)

With the coming of evening*,* Jesus arrived with the Twelve at the upstairs guest room. “The Twelve” refers to Jesus’ disciples as a group; ten arrived with Jesus, since two of them were already there ([14:13](https://ref.ly/Mark14:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:17


## Mark 14:18 (id: 21355)

As they were at the table: The Passover was eaten in a reclining position, as were other banquet meals. During the Passover meal, someone (usually the youngest son) would ask the host, “Why is this night different from other nights?” The father or host would then recount the stories of the Passover and the Exodus ([Deut 26:5–9](https://ref.ly/Deut26:5-Deut26:9)). The elements of the Passover meal were symbolic. The Passover lamb served as a reminder of the blood of the sacrificial lambs that protected Israelite homes from the angel of death, who visited the firstborn in Egypt ([Exod 12:28–30](https://ref.ly/Exod12:28-Exod12:30)). Unleavened bread recalled the rapid exodus of God’s people ([Exod 12:31–34](https://ref.ly/Exod12:31-Exod12:34), [39](https://ref.ly/Exod12:39)). The salt water represented the tears of their bondage and the crossing of the Red Sea, and the bitter herbs their slavery. The four cups of wine acknowledged God’s fourfold promise in [Exod 6:6–7](https://ref.ly/Exod6:6-Exod6:7). • The terrible deed of betraying Jesus was more heinous in that the betrayer was one of those eating with him (see [Pss 41:9](https://ref.ly/Ps41:9); [55:12–14](https://ref.ly/Ps55:12-Ps55:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Surely! (ID: `keyterm:Surely`)

## Mark 14:19 (id: 21356)

The disciples were shocked and saddened, asking, Am I the one? This is the first they had heard that Jesus would be betrayed (though the reader has known it since [3:19](https://ref.ly/Mark3:19)). Jesus maintained his betrayer’s anonymity.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:19


## Mark 14:21 (id: 21357)

must die, as the Scriptures declared: See, e.g., [Isa 52:13–53:12](https://ref.ly/Isa52:13-Isa53:12). Jesus foreknew this betrayal as part of the divine plan (cp. [8:31–33](https://ref.ly/Mark8:31-Mark8:33)), but his betrayer was condemned. Jesus and the Gospel writers do not explain how God’s sovereignty and Judas’s human responsibility can coexist, but both are stated without compromise (see [John 19:11](https://ref.ly/John19:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Disaster (ID: `keyterm:Disaster`)

## Mark 14:22–25 (id: 21358)

The Last Supper began with a blessing. In modern practice, Christians ask God to bless the food; Jews in Jesus’ day blessed God for providing the food with the words, “Blessed are you, O Lord our God, King of the universe, who brings forth bread from the earth.” • this is my body. . . . This is my blood: Some understand these words to mean that the bread and wine are transformed into the very body and blood of Jesus (*transubstantiation*); others, that the bread and wine remain bread and wine but that the real presence of Jesus is found in them (*consubstantiation*). In these two views, when one participates in the Lord’s Supper, one actually eats and drinks the body and blood of Jesus. A third view is that in eating the bread and wine, Christians spiritually feed on Jesus. A fourth view is that the meal is primarily a memorial, and the elements are symbolic of Jesus’ sacrifice on our behalf. See also [John 6:53–63](https://ref.ly/John6:53-John6:63) and corresponding study note.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:22-25
* **Associated Articles:** John 6:53-58 (ID: `19200@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Blood (ID: `keyterm:Blood`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Surely! (ID: `keyterm:Surely`); Body (ID: `keyterm:Body.3`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Mark 14:33–34 (id: 21366)

Jesus went ahead with Peter, James, and John ([5:37–43](https://ref.ly/Mark5:37-Mark5:43); [9:2–9](https://ref.ly/Mark9:2-Mark9:9); see also [13:3](https://ref.ly/Mark13:3)). They asked them to *keep watch* and pray with him, sharing in his distress.

## Mark 14:35–36 (id: 21367)

Jesus fell to the ground to pray because of his deep distress (see [Gen 17:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen17:1-Gen17:3); [Lev 9:24](https://ref.ly/Lev9:24); [Num 14:5](https://ref.ly/Num14:5); [16:4](https://ref.ly/Num16:4)). Since it was normal to pray out loud, Jesus’ prayer was probably overheard by the three disciples, who would not have fallen asleep immediately. • Jesus addressed God as Abba, Father*,* indicating their close relationship (see [Rom 8:15](https://ref.ly/Rom8:15); [Gal 4:6](https://ref.ly/Gal4:6)). • Please take this cup . . . from me: In faith, Jesus expressed his own feelings and desires to the Father. It was not only the physical agony of crucifixion that terrified him, but also the unique death that he would experience. He who knew no sin would experience the wrath of God against sin ([Mark 15:34](https://ref.ly/Mark15:34); [2 Cor 5:21](https://ref.ly/2Cor5:21); [Gal 3:13](https://ref.ly/Gal3:13)). • Yet I want your will to be done, not mine: Jesus’ submission to the Father’s will is a model for his followers.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:35-36

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 14:37–38 (id: 21368)

are you asleep? The question was a rebuke, for Jesus knew that Peter had been sleeping. • Though the spirit is willing to avoid temptation (see [Rom 7:18](https://ref.ly/Rom7:18), [22–23](https://ref.ly/Rom7:22-Rom7:23)), the body (literally *the flesh,* which denotes the general weakness and vulnerability of humanity) is weak.

## Mark 14:41–42 (id: 21370)

The content of Jesus’ prayer was probably the same as before (see [14:35–36](https://ref.ly/Mark14:35-Mark14:36), [39](https://ref.ly/Mark14:39)). The third failure of Peter, James, and John to watch and pray recalls Jesus’ prediction that Peter would deny him three times ([14:30](https://ref.ly/Mark14:30), [66–72](https://ref.ly/Mark14:66-Mark14:72)). • The words sleep and have your rest can be interpreted as a command, as in the NLT. Others take it as an exclamation (“You are sleeping and resting!”). Still others take it as a rhetorical question (“Are you sleeping and resting?”). • the time (literally *hour*) has come: The passion of Jesus had begun, the hour for pouring out the blood of the sacrificial lamb ([14:24](https://ref.ly/Mark14:24)). This statement coincided with the arrival of Judas and the armed crowd seeking to seize Jesus. Jesus was betrayed into the hands of sinners for whom he willingly went to the cross ([2:17](https://ref.ly/Mark2:17); [10:45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:45); [14:21](https://ref.ly/Mark14:21)). • Up, let’s be going: Jesus accepted the cup God had given him. He went out to defeat his enemies by dying for them.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:41-42


## Mark 14:43 (id: 21371)

Judas’s treachery in Jesus’ arrest is heightened by the reference to him as one of the twelve disciples. Having agreed with the leading priests to betray Jesus at the right time and place ([14:10–11](https://ref.ly/Mark14:10-Mark14:11)), he led an armed group to seize Jesus secretly at night apart from the people ([14:1–2](https://ref.ly/Mark14:1-Mark14:2)). Although they wanted to avoid a conflict, the crowd was large enough to quell any resistance by Jesus or his followers (cp. [Luke 22:52](https://ref.ly/Luke22:52); [John 18:3](https://ref.ly/John18:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judas (ID: `person:Judas.2`)

## Mark 14:44–45 (id: 21372)

Gethsemane was dark, and Jesus was personally unknown to most of the crowd sent to seize him (see [John 18:7–8](https://ref.ly/John18:7-John18:8)), so Judas had given a sign by which he would identify Jesus. Judas addressed Jesus as Rabbi and greeted him with a kiss*,* a common form of greeting ([1 Sam 10:1](https://ref.ly/1Sam10:1); [2 Sam 19:39](https://ref.ly/2Sam19:39); [Luke 7:45](https://ref.ly/Luke7:45)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:44-45

## Mark 14:53–54 (id: 21377)

The leading priests, the elders, and the teachers of religious law were not synonymous with the “entire high council” ([14:55](https://ref.ly/Mark14:55); [15:1](https://ref.ly/Mark15:1)), or Sanhedrin, but they made up a large part of it. Peter followed Jesus into the high priest’s courtyard*,* where the Sanhedrin was meeting. The stage is set for Peter’s denial ([14:66–72](https://ref.ly/Mark14:66-Mark14:72)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:53-54

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Peter (ID: `person:Peter`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Courtyard (ID: `realia:Courtyard`)

## Mark 14:53–65 (id: 21378)

The story of Jesus’ trial follows immediately upon his arrest. Objections have been raised as to the historicity of the various accounts of Jesus’ trial, because of differences of detail from the rules found in the Mishnah tractate *Mishnah Sanhedrin*. However, (1\) the Mishnah was written around AD 200, whereas the Gospel of Mark was written in the late 60s, over 130 years earlier; (2\) the rules found in *Mishnah Sanhedrin* idealize what later rabbis thought should take place in such trials and do not necessarily describe what did in fact take place; (3\) it is questionable whether the Sadducees leading the Sanhedrin would have followed the Pharisaic rules found in *Mishnah Sanhedrin* (see [Acts 23:6–10](https://ref.ly/Acts23:6-Acts23:10)); (4\) the rules found in *Mishnah Sanhedrin* sometimes conflict with what the Jewish historian Josephus wrote; (5\) existing laws of conduct were not necessarily followed—Jesus was being tried by a kangaroo court, in which the sentence was predetermined and only the charge for carrying it out was sought ([Mark 14:55](https://ref.ly/Mark14:55)); (6\) if we must choose between the trial accounts found in the Gospels and *Mishnah Sanhedrin*, there is no reason to choose the reliability of *Mishnah Sanhedrin* over that of the Gospels.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:53-65

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Witness (ID: `keyterm:Witness`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Condemn (ID: `keyterm:Condemn`); Blaspheme (ID: `keyterm:Blaspheme`); Peter (ID: `person:Peter`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Clothing (ID: `realia:Clothing`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Courtyard (ID: `realia:Courtyard`)

## Mark 14:55–59 (id: 21379)

The trial took place before the entire high council (Greek *Sanhedrin*), which had seventy members and was led by the high priest. The Gospels portray a formal trial: There was a search for witnesses ([14:55](https://ref.ly/Mark14:55)), eyewitness testimony ([14:56–59](https://ref.ly/Mark14:56-Mark14:59)), Jesus being placed under oath ([Matt 26:63](https://ref.ly/Matt26:63)), Jesus being allowed to defend himself ([Mark 14:60](https://ref.ly/Mark14:60)), the high priest tearing his robe ([14:63](https://ref.ly/Mark14:63)), and the concluding verdict by the Sanhedrin ([14:64](https://ref.ly/Mark14:64)). This does not mean that it was a fair trial—the decision to put Jesus to death had already been made. Evidence was not sought to determine the truth, but to obtain a guilty verdict and death sentence. • False witnesses giving false testimony misrepresented what Jesus said about the destruction and rebuilding of the Temple (see [15:29](https://ref.ly/Mark15:29); [John 2:19](https://ref.ly/John2:19); [Acts 6:14](https://ref.ly/Acts6:14)). Because the false witnesses contradicted each other*,* their testimony was unacceptable ([Num 35:30](https://ref.ly/Num35:30); [Deut 17:6](https://ref.ly/Deut17:6); [19:15](https://ref.ly/Deut19:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:55-59

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Mark 14:60–61 (id: 21381)

Because the false witnesses did not agree, the high priest asked Jesus for evidence against himself ([14:56](https://ref.ly/Mark14:56)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:60-61

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`)

## Mark 14:62 (id: 21382)

I Am: Cp. study note on 6:49–50. The reader has known from the beginning that Jesus was the Messiah, the Son of God ([1:1](https://ref.ly/Mark1:1)). This was acknowledged by demons ([1:24](https://ref.ly/Mark1:24); [3:11](https://ref.ly/Mark3:11); [5:7](https://ref.ly/Mark5:7)), by God ([1:11](https://ref.ly/Mark1:11); [9:7](https://ref.ly/Mark9:7)), and by the disciples ([8:29–30](https://ref.ly/Mark8:29-Mark8:30)), but this is the first time that Jesus openly and publicly acknowledged that he was the Messiah. • The second part of Jesus’ reply was that he would sit in the place of power at God’s right hand*,* which foretold his resurrection and ascension ([Luke 24:50–51](https://ref.ly/Luke24:50-Luke24:51); [Acts 1:9–11](https://ref.ly/Acts1:9-Acts1:11); see [Phil 2:9](https://ref.ly/Phil2:9); [Heb 1:3](https://ref.ly/Heb1:3)) and his triumphal coming on the clouds of heaven to judge the world. At Jesus’ return, roles will be reversed, and those judging the Son of Man will be judged by him.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:62
* **Associated Articles:** Mark 6:49-50 (ID: `21540@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`)

## Mark 14:63–64 (id: 21383)

The high priest tore his clothing at Jesus’ response. This was a judicial act that indicated a guilty verdict and signified that there was no need to find other witnesses. The rest of the Sanhedrin agreed that Jesus was guilty and deserved to die. • Exactly what was blasphemous in Jesus’ reply is unclear. Perhaps it was his reply “I Am,” which was the way God referred to himself in [Exod 3:14](https://ref.ly/Exod3:14) (see [John 8:58](https://ref.ly/John8:58)). However, Mark is not necessarily repeating the actual words Jesus said at his trial (cp. [Matt 26:64](https://ref.ly/Matt26:64); [Luke 22:70](https://ref.ly/Luke22:70)), so these words could simply be indicating Jesus’ affirmative response to the high priest’s question. He was the Messiah, the Son of God. During the second Jewish revolt against Rome, Bar Kokhba (AD 132–135\) claimed to be the Messiah, and it was not considered blasphemous. Jesus’ identification of himself as the Son of Man cannot have been considered blasphemous, for we have over fifty instances before Jesus’ trial in which he used this title, and the charge of blasphemy was never raised. Yet it was too much for the high priest and the Sanhedrin when Jesus clearly claimed to be the Son of Man of [Dan 7:13](https://ref.ly/Dan7:13) coming in God’s name to judge the world. This declaration was also added to the fact that, during his ministry, Jesus forgave sins ([Mark 2:5–7](https://ref.ly/Mark2:5-Mark2:7); [Luke 7:48–50](https://ref.ly/Luke7:48-Luke7:50)), claimed to be the Son of God ([Mark 12:6](https://ref.ly/Mark12:6)), pronounced judgment upon the Temple ([14:58](https://ref.ly/Mark14:58)), and claimed to be Lord of the Sabbath ([2:28](https://ref.ly/Mark2:28)). Already convinced that Jesus should be put to death, the religious leaders now pronounced the predetermined guilty verdict ([14:55](https://ref.ly/Mark14:55)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 14:63-64

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Witness (ID: `keyterm:Witness`); Blaspheme (ID: `keyterm:Blaspheme`); Condemn (ID: `keyterm:Condemn`); Clothing (ID: `realia:Clothing`)

## Mark 14:72 (id: 21390)

When Peter denied Jesus for the third time, the rooster crowed again. This fulfilled the prediction of Jesus from [14:30](https://ref.ly/Mark14:30). 

While Jesus stood bravely before the Sanhedrin (the Jewish ruling council), Peter became afraid of people with little power. He called down curses on himself and said he did not know Jesus. When Peter remembered what Jesus had said, he began to weep.

## Mark 15:1 (id: 21399)

* *Early in the morning*: Roman trials usually began at the start of the day. It is uncertain if this was a second meeting of *the whole Sanhedrin* or if it refers back to the council's decision in [14:63–64](https://ref.ly/Mark14:63-Mark14:64) and resumes here.
* Since the *Sanhedrin* could not impose capital punishment ([John 18:31](https://ref.ly/John18:31)), they took Jesus to Pilate, the Roman governor of Judea from AD 26–36\. Pilate usually lived on the coast at Caesarea. But he stayed in Jerusalem during Passover. This was when Israel's celebration of the exodus event increased hopes for freedom from Roman rule.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pilate (ID: `person:Pilate`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`)

## Mark 15:2 (id: 21407)

* The phrase *King of the Jews* is similar to the Jewish title "King of Israel" in [15:32](https://ref.ly/Mark15:32).
* *You have said so*: The response Jesus gives to Pilate's question appears in all four Gospels. Jesus clearly understood himself to be a king ([10:37–38](https://ref.ly/Mark10:37-Mark10:38); [11:9–10](https://ref.ly/Mark11:9-Mark11:10); [12:35–37](https://ref.ly/Mark12:35-Mark12:37)). Pilate eventually crucified him for this reason ([15:26](https://ref.ly/Mark15:26)). But Pilate knew Jesus was not a political threat ([John 18:33–39](https://ref.ly/John18:33-John18:39)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:2

## Mark 15:6–8 (id: 21419)

* The *custom* described in [15:6–8](https://ref.ly/Mark15:6-Mark15:8) is not known outside of the Gospels, but all four Gospels mention it ([Matthew 27:15–21](https://ref.ly/Matt27:15-Matt27:21); [Luke 23:18–25](https://ref.ly/Luke23:18-Luke23:25); [John 18:37–40](https://ref.ly/John18:37-John18:40)). In many cases, ancient societies released prisoners during special holidays.

* *who had committed murder during the insurrection*: Pilate's willingness to release Barabbas likely shows that Barabbas had not killed Roman citizens or soldiers ([Matthew 15:15](https://ref.ly/Matt15:15)).

## Mark 15:15 (id: 21394)

* *To satisfy the crowd*: To protect his job, Pilate ordered the crucifixion of an innocent man.
* *he had Jesus flogged*: Flogging (severe beating) usually came before crucifixion. Soldiers used leather straps with pieces of metal, stone, or bone attached. These blows tore the skin and could even cause death.

    This fulfilled what Jesus had predicted earlier in [10:34](https://ref.ly/Mark10:34).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:15

## Mark 15:21–22 (id: 21400)

* After they left the walled city of Jerusalem, Jesus became too weak to carry his cross. The Roman soldiers forced *Simon of Cyrene* (a city in northern Africa) to carry it (see [Matthew 5:41](https://ref.ly/Matt5:41)).

    Usually, a person being crucified had to carry the horizontal crossbeam (called the *patibulum*) to the place of execution. The vertical post (called the *stipes*) was often left in the ground as a warning to others (like a hangman’s scaffold in a public square).

* *Simon of Cyrene, the father of Alexander and Rufus*: These men were probably known to Mark's audience (see [Romans 16:13](https://ref.ly/Rom16:13)).
* *Golgotha* is an Aramaic word, meaning *The Place of the Skull*. In the Latin Vulgate, "skull" is *calvariae* ("Calvary"). In the time of Jesus, Golgotha was outside the walls of Jerusalem ([John 19:20](https://ref.ly/John19:20); see [Matthew 27:32](https://ref.ly/Matt27:32); [Hebrews 13:12](https://ref.ly/Heb13:12)). When Herod Antipas later enlarged the city with the so\-called third wall, Golgotha was enclosed.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:21-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rufus (ID: `person:Rufus`); Cyrene (ID: `place:Cyrene`); Alexander (ID: `person:Alexander`); Golgotha (ID: `place:Golgotha`); Simon (ID: `person:Simon.5`); Cross (ID: `realia:Cross`)

## Mark 15:23 (id: 21401)

* It is uncertain if people offered Jesus *wine mixed with myrrh* as a kind act or to mock him ([Proverbs 31:6](https://ref.ly/Prov31:6)). Jesus refused the calming drink mixture because he had committed himself to accept the suffering God had given him ([Mark 10:38–39](https://ref.ly/Mark10:38-Mark10:39); [14:36](https://ref.ly/Mark14:36)).
* People have used myrrh since before the time of Jesus to treat wounds, infections, and digestive problems.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Myrrhed Wine (ID: `realia:MyrrhedWine`); Myrrh (ID: `flora:Myrrh`)

## Mark 15:24 (id: 21402)

* Crucifixion began with the Medes and Persians in the 600s BC. It spread to the eastern Mediterranean in the 300s BC through Alexander the Great. It became the main form of capital punishment in the Roman Empire until AD 337, when Constantine banned the practice.

    Crucifixion was slow, shameful, and full of pain. Sometimes a victim stayed alive for several days. Birds and dogs might even begin to eat the body before the person died. A victim could be tied to the cross with ropes or fastened with nails. Nails were used in the crucifixion of Jesus ([Luke 24:39](https://ref.ly/Luke24:39); [John 20:25](https://ref.ly/John20:25), [27](https://ref.ly/John20:27); see [Colossians 2:14](https://ref.ly/Col2:14)). To prevent early death from loss of breathing, a place to rest the feet or a seat was often added to the post.

* All four Gospels report that the soldiers *divided his garments* among themselves.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Clothing (ID: `realia:Clothing`); Cross (ID: `realia:Cross`); Lot (ID: `realia:Lot`)

## Mark 15:25 (id: 21403)

Mark states that they crucified Jesus at *the third hour*, which is nine o'clock in the morning. [John 19:14](https://ref.ly/John19:14) mentions the time as around the sixth hour, or noon. These are approximate times. They would often describe events in the late morning as happening around the third hour ([Matthew 20:3](https://ref.ly/Matt20:3); [Acts 2:15](https://ref.ly/Acts2:15)) or sixth hour ([Mark 15:33](https://ref.ly/Mark15:33); [Matthew 20:5](https://ref.ly/Matt20:5); [27:45](https://ref.ly/Matt27:45); [Luke 23:44](https://ref.ly/Luke23:44); [John 4:6](https://ref.ly/John4:6); [19:14](https://ref.ly/John19:14); [Acts 10:9](https://ref.ly/Acts10:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cross (ID: `realia:Cross`)

## Mark 15:33 (id: 21410)

*From* *the sixth hour* *until* *the ninth hour darkness came over* *all* *the land*: The whole land likely refers to all of Judah. The *darkness* was both real and symbolic. It showed the evil nature of what was happening and gave a sign of the judgment that Jesus said would come on Israel (see [13:1–31](https://ref.ly/Mark13:1-Mark13:31); [14:58](https://ref.ly/Mark14:58); [15:29](https://ref.ly/Mark15:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:33


## Mark 15:34 (id: 21411)

* *Eloi, Eloi … why have You forsaken me*: Jesus cried out loudly. Mark records his words in Aramaic (the language Jesus spoke) and then gives the meaning in Greek.
* *"My God, My God, why have you forsaken me?"*: Jesus is quoting [Psalm 22:1](https://ref.ly/Ps22:1). This statement should be understood by considering [Mark 14:27](https://ref.ly/Mark14:27), [Psalm 22](https://ref.ly/Ps22:1-Ps22:31), [Isaiah 53:10](https://ref.ly/Isa53:10), [2 Corinthians 5:21](https://ref.ly/2Cor5:21), and [Galatians 3:13](https://ref.ly/Gal3:13). At this moment, God’s purpose was being fulfilled. Jesus was giving his life as “a ransom for many” ([Mark 10:45](https://ref.ly/Mark10:45)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 15:35–36 (id: 21412)

The Aramaic word *Eloi* and the Hebrew form *Eli* ([Matthew 27:46](https://ref.ly/Matt27:46)) sound similar to the name Elijah. Because of this, some people standing nearby thought Jesus was calling for the prophet Elijah to come and rescue him (see [Malachi 4:5](https://ref.ly/Mal4:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:35-36

The death of Jesus, like the crucifixion, is told with stark simplicity. The Gospel account gives only a few direct details.

## Mark 15:38 (id: 21414)

The *veil* *of the temple* … *torn* *in two* might have been one of two curtains. It may have been the curtain that separated the sanctuary from the courtyard. This curtain was a large and beautiful tapestry about 23 meters tall (80 feet). Or it may have been the inner curtain that separated the Most Holy Place (the most sacred room) from the rest of the sanctuary.

If it was the outer curtain, people could have seen it tear (compare the "darkness" in [15:33](https://ref.ly/Mark15:33)). This would show that the prediction of Jesus about the destruction of the sanctuary ([14:58](https://ref.ly/Mark14:58); [15:29](https://ref.ly/Mark15:29)) was already beginning to happen in a spiritual way (see [Rom 9–11](https://ref.ly/Rom9:1-Rom11:36)). The full physical destruction would come later, in AD 70\.

If it was the inner curtain, the tearing likely had a different meaning. Just as the heavens opened at the baptism of Jesus to show his direct access to God (see study note on Mark 1:10), his death now made that access possible for his followers (see [Hebrews 6:19–20](https://ref.ly/Heb6:19-Heb6:20); [9:3–14](https://ref.ly/Heb9:3-Heb9:14); [10:19–20](https://ref.ly/Heb10:19-Heb10:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:38
* **Associated Articles:** Mark 1:10 (ID: `21186@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Curtain (ID: `realia:Curtain`)

## Mark 15:39 (id: 21415)

* *The centurion*: The Latin word *centurion* refers to an officer in charge of 100 men.
* Jesus as the *Son of God* was announced by many voices. God declared it([1:11](https://ref.ly/Mark1:11); [9:7](https://ref.ly/Mark9:7)). Demons also recognized it ([1:24](https://ref.ly/Mark1:24), [34](https://ref.ly/Mark1:34); [3:11](https://ref.ly/Mark3:11); [5:7](https://ref.ly/Mark5:7)). Mark states it at the beginning of the Gospel ([1:1](https://ref.ly/Mark1:1)). Jesus himself speaks about it ([12:6](https://ref.ly/Mark12:6); [13:32](https://ref.ly/Mark13:32); [14:61–62](https://ref.ly/Mark14:61-Mark14:62)). Now, a Gentile (non\-Jewish) officer also declares it.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:39

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Centurion at Crucifixion (ID: `person:Centurion.3`); True (ID: `keyterm:True`)

## Mark 15:40–41 (id: 21416)

* These *women* helped meet some of the financial needs of Jesus (see [Luke 8:2–3](https://ref.ly/Luke8:2-Luke8:3)). They were present at the burial and the empty tomb ([Mark 15:47](https://ref.ly/Mark15:47); [16:1–8](https://ref.ly/Mark16:1-Mark16:8)). They were the first to witness the resurrection of Jesus.
* Mary Magdalene was from the village of Magdala near the Sea of Galilee. She is a key figure in the resurrection stories ([16:1](https://ref.ly/Mark16:1), [9](https://ref.ly/Mark16:9); [Matthew 28:1](https://ref.ly/Matt28:1); [Luke 24:10](https://ref.ly/Luke24:10); [John 20:1](https://ref.ly/John20:1), [11–18](https://ref.ly/John20:11-John20:18)).
* *Mary the mother of James the younger*: This James might have been James son of Alphaeus ([Mark 3:18](https://ref.ly/Mark3:18)).
* Salome is mentioned only here and in [Mark 16:1](https://ref.ly/Mark16:1).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:40-41

## Mark 15:43–45 (id: 21417)

*Joseph of Arimathea:* He was possibly from Ramathaim, a village about 32 kilometers (20 miles) northwest of Jerusalem. A respected member of the Jewish high council (Sanhedrin), he was also a secret follower of Jesus. He was waiting for the kingdom of God to come (see [Matthew 27:57](https://ref.ly/Matt27:57); [Luke 23:50–51](https://ref.ly/Luke23:50-Luke23:51); [John 19:38](https://ref.ly/John19:38)). He bravely asked Pilate for the body of Jesus so he could bury it. 

As *a prominent Council member* who was not known as a disciple, his request supported Rome's interests. This addressed Jewish concerns about leaving the dead exposed after sunset ([Deuteronomy 21:22–23](https://ref.ly/Deut21:22-Deut21:23)). It also prevented the disciples of Jesus from receiving the body.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:43-45

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.6`); Pilate (ID: `person:Pilate`); Arimathea (ID: `place:Arimathea`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Body (ID: `keyterm:Body.3`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Mark 15:46 (id: 21418)

Joseph prepared the body of Jesus for burial and laid it in his own tomb (see [Matthew 27:60](https://ref.ly/Matt27:60)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 15:46

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Grave (ID: `realia:Grave`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`)

## Mark 16:1 (id: 21421)

* At sunset, when the Sabbath day ended, the shops opened again. The women "bought spices so they could go and anoint the body of Jesus" ([16:1](https://ref.ly/Mark16:1)). They intended to reduce the smell of the dead body.
* The women did not expect Jesus to resurrect from the dead. Even the empty tomb did not convince them of his resurrection ([John 20:2](https://ref.ly/John20:2), [11–15](https://ref.ly/John20:11-John20:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 16:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Salome (ID: `person:Salome`); Mary (ID: `person:Mary.2`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Anointed (ID: `keyterm:Anointed`); Mary Magdalene (ID: `person:Marymagdalene`); James (ID: `person:James.3`); Aloes (ID: `realia:Aloes`)

## Mark 16:3–4 (id: 21422)

The women wondered how to move the large stone covering the tomb. God had already moved the stone to let them and the other disciples enter.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 16:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Grave (ID: `realia:Grave`)

## Mark 16:7 (id: 21423)

The angel’s message to the disciples repeated what Jesus had already said ([14:28](https://ref.ly/Mark14:28)). It reminded them of his promise.

The disciples had abandoned Jesus and denied him, but they would be forgiven. Their role as apostles (messengers sent by Jesus) would be restored.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 16:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Peter (ID: `person:Peter`); To Command (ID: `keyterm:Command`); Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Galilee (ID: `place:Galilee`)

## Mark 16:8 (id: 21424)

* The story ends with the women running away from the tomb. They were shaking with fear and confused (not sure what was happening).
* *they did not say a word to anyone*: This can be understood in two ways. It may be positive. The women may not have spoken to others because they were focused on their task to tell the disciples ([16:7](https://ref.ly/Mark16:7); compare [Luke 10:4](https://ref.ly/Luke10:4); [2 Kings 4:29](https://ref.ly/2Kgs4:29)).

    Or it may be negative. The women may have failed to deliver the message at that moment (compare [Luke 24:5–11](https://ref.ly/Luke24:5-Luke24:11); [John 20:1–2](https://ref.ly/John20:1-John20:2), [18](https://ref.ly/John20:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 16:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Imperishable (ID: `keyterm:Imperishable`); Surely! (ID: `keyterm:Surely`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Grave (ID: `realia:Grave`)

## Mark 16:9–20 (id: 21425)

Nearly all scholars agree that Mark did not write the shorter ending and the longer ending of his Gospel. These endings use different styles, vocabulary, and theology (teaching about God). Also, the two best early Greek manuscripts (*Codex Sinaiticus* and *Codex Vaticanus*) do not include these endings.

However, many scholars also doubt that Mark meant to end his Gospel at [16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark16:8). Several reasons support this view: 

1. Mark emphasizes that the predictions of Jesus are fulfilled. If the Gospel ends at [16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark16:8), there is no account of Jesus appearing after his resurrection.
2. All the other Gospels include stories of Jesus appearing to the women and the disciples.
3. Some early manuscripts include added endings. This suggests that some early copyists (people who copied texts by hand) thought the Gospel needed a clearer ending.
4. There is no clear reason why Mark would choose to end at [16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark16:8). Many suggested reasons come from modern ways of reading, not from how a first\-century Christian writer would likely think.
5. The Gospel begins with a strong statement that Jesus is the Messiah ([1:1](https://ref.ly/Mark1:1)), but ending at [16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark16:8) would leave the story with fear instead of resolution.
6. The Greek word *gar* (“because”) appears at the end of [16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark16:8). It is very unusual for an ancient Greek book to end with this word.
7. [Mark 16:7](https://ref.ly/Mark16:7) suggests the disciples will meet Jesus in Galilee. If [16:8](https://ref.ly/Mark16:8) was the original ending, it would be the only prediction in the Gospel that did not come true.

Because of these reasons, many scholars think the original ending was lost (for example, the last page may have been damaged or removed) or that Mark did not finish writing it.

* **Associated Passages:** Mark 16:9-20
